「做父母的很少真正明智，而那些有良心的人偏要在孩子和家庭女教师面前装出明智模样，这份紧绷必定苦不堪言。牧师也并不比旁人更虔诚，却不得不时时刻刻在羊群面前摆出这副架子。至于家庭女教师，米诺拉小姐，我敢断言——我知道自己在说什么——天下没有比这更难以忍受的事了：对那些一举一动都把弱点和愚蠢暴露无遗的人，你还得彬彬有礼，甚至低声下气；因为孩子和雇主在场，你还得端出一副与你内心毫不相称的尊严架子。一位板着脸的一家之父——他做单身汉时多半最不体面——在自己的餐桌旁是很值得玩味的研究对象：仅仅因为孩子们正望着他，他就不得不摆出永远正确的派头。为人父母这个事实，并不能赋予他任何至高而骤降的美德；而且我可以向你担保，在盯着他的那些眼睛里，最挑剔、最觉好玩的，恰恰是那位屈就家庭女教师之职的卑微之人。」

「哦，琼斯小姐，说得太妙了！」我们听见米诺拉用狂喜的声调说，而我们却被这些高论吓得呆若木鸡。「您介意我把它记进我的书里吗？您说得实在太漂亮了。」

「若没有几个小时的放松，」琼斯小姐继续说，「没有对当众展示的辛苦德行的私下补偿，谁能蹚得过日复一日的行为端正？那就没有回旋，没有余地让更好的冲动生长，没有忏悔的容身之地。父母、牧师和家庭女教师们，就会落到那位胖太太的境地——永远没有一刻安生，好让她把束身胸衣解下来。」

===Original===
“My dear, what a firebrand!” whispered Irais. I got up and went in. They were sitting on the sofa, Minora with clasped hands, gazing admiringly into Miss Jones’s face, which wore a very different expression from the one of sour and unwilling propriety I have been used to seeing.

“May I ask you to come to tea?” I said to Minora. “And I should like to have the children a little while.”

She got up very reluctantly, but I waited with the door open until she had gone in and the two babies had followed. They had been playing at stuffing each other’s ears with pieces of newspaper while Miss Jones provided Minora with noble thoughts for her work, and had to be tortured afterward with tweezers. I said nothing to Minora, but kept her with us till dinnertime, and this morning we went for a long sleigh-drive. When we came in to lunch there was no Miss Jones.

===Chinese===
「乖乖，好一个煽动家！」伊蕾斯耳语道。我起身走进去。她们坐在沙发上，米诺拉双手紧握，倾慕地凝视着琼斯小姐的脸，那张脸上的表情，同我平素看惯的那副酸溜溜、勉勉强强的规矩模样，真是判若两人。

「可以请你来喝茶吗？」我对米诺拉说。「孩子们我也想留一小会儿。」

她老大不情愿地站起来，但我扶着敞开的门等着，直到她进去、两个宝贝也跟了进去。方才琼斯小姐为米诺拉的大作供应崇高思想的时候，她们俩一直在玩往彼此耳朵里塞报纸的游戏，事后不得不用镊子受刑。我对米诺拉什么也没说，只把她留在我们身边一直到晚饭时分，今天早上我们坐着雪橇出去兜了一大圈。等我们回来吃午饭，琼斯小姐已经不在了。

===Original===
“Is Miss Jones ill?” asked Minora.

“She is gone,” I said.

“Gone?”

“Did you never hear of such things as sick mothers?” asked Irais blandly; and we talked resolutely of something else.

All the afternoon Minora has moped. She had found a kindred spirit, and it has been ruthlessly torn from her arms as kindred spirits so often are. It is enough to make her mope, and it is not her fault, poor thing, that she should have preferred the society of a Miss Jones to that of Irais and myself.

===Chinese===
「琼斯小姐病了吗？」米诺拉问。

「她走了。」我说。

「走了？」

「生病的母亲这类事情，你难道从来没听说过？」伊蕾斯不动声色地问；随后我们斩钉截铁地谈起了别的。

整个下午米诺拉都闷闷不乐。她好不容易找到一个知音，却被无情地从她怀里夺走了——知音们常常落得这个下场。这足够让她发闷的了；她宁可亲近一位琼斯小姐，而不亲近伊蕾斯和我，这也怪不得她，可怜的人。

===Original===
At dinner Irais surveyed her with her head on one side. “You look so pale,” she said; “are you not well?”

Minora raised her eyes heavily, with the patient air of one who likes to be thought a sufferer. “I have a slight headache,” she replied gently.

“I hope you are not going to be ill,” said Irais with great concern, “because there is only a cow-doctor to be had here, and though he means well, I believe he is rather rough.” Minora was plainly startled. “But what do you do if you are ill?” she asked.

“Oh, we are never ill,” said I; “the very knowledge that there would be no one to cure us seems to keep us healthy.”

“And if anyone takes to her bed,” said Irais, “Elizabeth always calls in the cow-doctor.”

Minora was silent. She feels, I am sure, that she has got into a part of the world peopled solely by barbarians, and that the only civilised creature besides herself has departed and left her at our mercy. Whatever her reflections may be her symptoms are visibly abating.

===Chinese===
晚餐时，伊蕾斯歪着头打量她。「你脸色这么苍白，」她说，「是不是不舒服？」

米诺拉沉沉地抬起眼，带着喜欢被当作受苦者的忍耐神气。「我有一点头痛。」她轻轻答道。

「我劝你可千万别病倒，」伊蕾斯万分关切地说，「因为这儿能请到的只有一位牛医，而且尽管他心是好的，我相信他手挺重。」米诺拉分明吓了一跳。「那你们病了怎么办呢？」她问。

「哦，我们从来不生病，」我说；「一想到病了也没人给治，这似乎就让我们保持健康了。」

「万一有谁卧床不起，」伊蕾斯说，「伊丽莎白总是把牛医请来。」

米诺拉不作声了。我敢肯定，她觉得自己落到了世界上一个只住着野蛮人的角落，而除了她之外唯一的文明生物已经离去，把她丢下任凭我们发落。不管她心里在想什么，她的病征眼见着消退了。

## January 1st / 1月1日

===Original===
The service on New Year’s Eve is the only one in the whole year that in the least impresses me in our little church, and then the very bareness and ugliness of the place and the ceremonial produce an effect that a snug service in a well-lit church never would. Last night we took Irais and Minora, and drove the three lonely miles in a sleigh. It was pitch-dark, and blowing great guns. We sat wrapped up to our eyes in furs, and as mute as a funeral procession.

===Chinese===
除夕夜的礼拜，是我们小教堂全年之中唯一能让我有丝毫触动的；而且恰是那地方的简陋丑朴、那仪式的粗陋，产生出灯火融融的教堂里一场舒舒服服的礼拜所绝不可能有的效果。昨晚我们带上了伊蕾斯和米诺拉，乘雪橇驶过那三英里荒凉的路。天黑得伸手不见五指，狂风怒号。我们裹着毛皮直裹到眼睛，一路缄默得像一支送葬的队伍。

===Original===
“We are going to the burial of our last year’s sins,” said Irais, as we started; and there certainly was a funereal sort of feeling in the air. Up in our gallery pew we tried to decipher our chorales by the light of the spluttering tallow candles stuck in holes in the woodwork, the flames wildly blown about by the draughts. The wind banged against the windows in great gusts, screaming louder than the organ, and threatening to blow out the agitated lights together. The parson in his gloomy pulpit, surrounded by a framework of dusty carved angels, took on an awful appearance of menacing Authority as he raised his voice to make himself heard above the clatter. Sitting there in the dark, I felt very small, and solitary, and defenceless, alone in a great, big, black world. The church was as cold as a tomb; some of the candles guttered and went out; the parson in his black robe spoke of death and judgment; I thought I heard a child’s voice screaming, and could hardly believe it was only the wind, and felt uneasy and full of forebodings; all my faith and philosophy deserted me, and I had a horrid feeling that I should probably be well punished, though for what I had no precise idea. If it had not been so dark, and if the wind had not howled so despairingly, I should have paid little attention to the threats issuing from the pulpit; but, as it was, I fell to making good resolutions. This is always a bad sign—only those who break them make them; and if you simply do as a matter of course that which is right as it comes, any preparatory resolving to do so becomes completely superfluous. I have for some years past left off making them on New Year’s Eve, and only the gale happening as it did reduced me to doing so last night; for I have long since discovered that, though the year and the resolutions may be new, I myself am not, and it is worse than useless putting new wine into old bottles.

===Chinese===
「我们这是去给去年的罪下葬呢。」启程时伊蕾斯说；空气里确确实实有一种送葬的气息。在楼座的专席上，我们借着插在木构件孔洞里、毕毕剥剥溅着火星的牛油烛，费力辨认圣歌的词谱，烛火被穿堂风吹得东倒西歪。风一阵阵撞在窗上，尖啸得盖过了管风琴，眼看要把那些惊惶的烛光一齐吹灭。牧师在他那阴沉沉的讲道坛上，被一圈积灰的木雕天使环绕着，提高嗓门好让人在这片喧闹之上听得见他，那一副威慑的威权面目显得可怕起来。坐在那黑暗里，我感到自己何其渺小、孤单、无遮无拦，独自处在一个巨大漆黑的世界中。教堂冷得像座坟墓；有几支烛淌了油熄灭了；黑袍牧师讲着死亡与审判；我仿佛听见一个孩子的尖叫声，简直不敢相信那只是风，心里忐忑，满是凶险的预感；我全部的信仰和哲思都弃我而去，只莫名恐惧地觉得自己多半要受重罚，虽则究竟为何受罚，我毫无确切的观念。若不是天那么黑，若不是风嚎得那么绝望，讲道坛上发出的那些恫吓我本不会理会；可是当时那般光景，我竟着手立起善愿来。这永远是个坏兆头——只有会违背善愿的人才立善愿；倘若你只是遇事顺理成章地做对的，那预备性的立志便纯属多余。这几年来我已不在除夕夜立善愿，昨夜若不是恰好刮了那场大风，也不至于落得再立一回；因为我早就发现，年岁和善愿尽可以是新的，我自己却不是，把新酒装进旧皮袋，非但无用，更糟。

===Original===
“But I am not an old bottle,” said Irais indignantly, when I held forth to her to the above effect a few hours later in the library, restored to all my philosophy by the warmth and light, “and I find my resolutions carry me very nicely into the spring. I revise them at the end of each month, and strike out the unnecessary ones. By the end of April they have been so severely revised that there are none left.”

“There, you see I am right; if you were not an old bottle your new contents would gradually arrange themselves amiably as a part of you, and the practice of your resolutions would lose its bitterness by becoming a habit.”

She shook her head. “Such things never lose their bitterness,” she said, “and that is why I don’t let them cling to me right into the summer. When May comes, I give myself up to jollity with all the rest of the world, and am too busy being happy to bother about anything I may have resolved when the days were cold and dark.”

“And that is just why I love you,” I thought. She often says what I feel.

===Chinese===
「可我不是旧皮袋，」几个小时后，我在书房里向伊蕾斯大发这番议论时——那时温暖和光明已把我全部的哲思都还了回来——她愤愤地说，「而且我发现我的善愿把我舒舒服服地一直送进春天。每到月底我就修订一遍，把不必要的划掉。到了四月底，它们已被修订得如此严厉，一条也不剩了。」

「瞧，这正说明我是对的；你若不是旧皮袋，那些新东西自会渐渐和顺地安顿下来，成为你的一部分，善愿的实行也会因成为习惯而失其苦味。」

她摇头。「这种事永远不会失其苦味，」她说，「所以我才不让它们黏缠着我一路拖进夏天。五月一到，我便同世上其余的人一道纵情行乐，忙着快活都来不及，哪还管天冷日短时节立过什么愿。」

「这正是我爱你的缘故。」我心里想。她常常说出我的心里话。
===Original===
“I wonder,” she went on after a pause, “whether men ever make resolutions?”

“I don’t think they do. Only women indulge in such luxuries. It is a nice sort of feeling, when you have nothing else to do, giving way to endless grief and penitence, and steeping yourself to the eyes in contrition; but it is silly. Why cry over things that are done? Why do naughty things at all, if you are going to repent afterward? Nobody is naughty unless they like being naughty; and nobody ever really repents unless they are afraid they are going to be found out.”

“By ‘nobody’ of course you mean women,” said Irais.

“Naturally; the terms are synonymous. Besides, men generally have the courage of their opinions.”

“I hope you are listening, Miss Minora,” said Irais in the amiably polite tone she assumes whenever she speaks to that young person.

It was getting on towards midnight, and we were sitting round the fire, waiting for the New Year, and sipping *Glühwein*, prepared at a small table by the Man of Wrath. It was hot, and sweet, and rather nasty, but it is proper to drink it on this one night, so of course we did.

===Chinese===
「我倒纳闷，」她顿了顿又接着说，「男人到底立不立善愿？」

「我看他们不立。只有女人才沉溺于这等奢侈。闲来无事，纵情于无尽的悲悔，把自己从头到脚泡在忏悔里，那滋味确实不错；可是很傻。做过的事何必哭？倘若事后还要悔，那又何必去做坏事？人若不乐意使坏，就不会使坏；人若不怕事情败露，就绝不会真心悔改。」

「你这『人』当然指的是女人。」伊蕾斯说。

「自然；这两个词本是同义。再说，男人大抵有勇气坚持自己的见解。」

「希望你听着呢，米诺拉小姐。」伊蕾斯说，用的是她每次对那位年轻人说话时惯有的那副和蔼客气的口吻。

时间渐近午夜，我们围炉而坐，等候新年，啜着愤怒之人在一张小桌旁调制出来的热红酒（Glühwein）。它滚烫、甜腻，味道相当不怎么样，但唯独这一夜喝它才算得体，所以我们自然照喝。

===Original===
Minora does not like either Irais or myself. We very soon discovered that, and laugh about it when we are alone together. I can understand her disliking Irais, but she must be a perverse creature not to like me. Irais has poked fun at her, and I have been, I hope, very kind; yet we are bracketed together in her black books. It is also apparent that she looks upon the Man of Wrath as an interesting example of an ill-used and misunderstood husband, and she is disposed to take him under her wing, and defend him on all occasions against us. He never speaks to her; he is at all times a man of few words, but, as far as Minora is concerned, he might have no tongue at all, and sits sphinx-like and impenetrable while she takes us to task about some remark of a profane nature that we may have addressed to him. One night, some days after her arrival, she developed a skittishness of manner which has since disappeared, and tried to be playful with him; but you might as well try to be playful with a graven image. The wife of one of the servants had just produced a boy, the first after a series of five daughters, and at dinner we drank the health of all parties concerned, the Man of Wrath making the happy father drink a glass off at one gulp, his heels well together in military fashion. Minora thought the incident typical of German manners, and not only made notes about it, but joined heartily in the health-drinking, and afterward grew skittish.

===Chinese===
米诺拉既不喜欢伊蕾斯，也不喜欢我。这一点我们很快就发现了，独处时还拿它取笑。她不喜欢伊蕾斯，我还能理解；可她居然不喜欢我，那她必是个拧巴的造物了。伊蕾斯挖苦过她，而我，我希望，一直非常和善；然而在她的黑名单上，我们却被并排登记在一起。同样显而易见的是，她把愤怒之人看作一个饱受虐待、无人理解的丈夫的有趣典型，有心把他收于自己的羽翼之下，在一切场合替他辩护，对抗我们。他从不跟她说话；他一向是个寡言的人，但在米诺拉这方面说来，他干脆可以没有舌头；她为着我们也许对他说过的某句大不敬之语而数落我们时，他便斯芬克斯一般坐着，高深莫测。她来后没几天的一个晚上，忽然生出一股欢蹦乱跳的劲儿——如今这劲儿已经无影无踪——想跟他顽笑；可你想跟一尊石雕的神像顽笑，也不过如此。一个仆人的妻子刚生下一个男孩，是接连五个女儿之后的头一个，晚餐时我们为诸位相关人等祝了健康，愤怒之人让那位快活的父亲把一杯酒一口干了，两脚跟并拢，一副军人做派。米诺拉认为这一幕颇具德国风俗的典型性，不但记了笔记，还由衷加入了祝酒，随后便欢腾起来。

===Original===
She proposed, first of all, to teach us a dance called, I think, the Washington Post, and which was, she said, much danced in England; and, to induce us to learn, she played the tune to us on the piano. We remained untouched by its beauties, each buried in an easy-chair toasting our toes at the fire. Amongst those toes were those of the Man of Wrath, who sat peaceably reading a book and smoking. Minora volunteered to show us the steps, and as we still did not move, danced solitary behind our chairs. Irais did not even turn her head to look, and I was the only one amiable or polite enough to do so. Do I deserve to be placed in Minora’s list of disagreeable people side by side with Irais? Certainly not. Yet I most surely am.

===Chinese===
她头一个提议，是要教我们一支舞，名字好像叫「华盛顿邮报」（Washington Post），说它在英格兰风行得很；为了劝我们学，她还在钢琴上给我们弹了那支曲子。那曲子的美妙没能打动我们，我们各自深陷在安乐椅里，对着炉火烘脚。那些脚趾当中，也有愤怒之人的，他安安静静坐着看书抽烟。米诺拉自告奋勇示范舞步，见我们仍旧不动，便独自在我们椅子后面跳了起来。伊蕾斯连头都懒得回一下，只有我一个人和善客气，肯看上一眼。我难道配被列入米诺拉那张讨厌鬼名单、与伊蕾斯并排吗？自然不配。可我千真万确被列进去了。
===Original===
“It wants the music, of course,” observed Minora breathlessly, darting in and out between the chairs, apparently addressing me, but glancing at the Man of Wrath.

No answer from anybody.

“It is *such* a pretty dance,” she panted again, after a few more gyrations.

No answer.

“And is all the rage at home.”

No answer.

“Do let me teach you. Won’t *you* try, Herr Sage?”

She went up to him and dropped him a little curtesy. It is thus she always addresses him, entirely oblivious to the fact, so patent to everyone else, that he resents it.

===Chinese===
「这当然得有音乐伴奏。」米诺拉上气不接下气地议论道，在椅子间穿进穿出，明面上是对我说，眼风却扫向愤怒之人。

谁也没有应声。

「这支舞可真好看。」又旋转了几圈之后，她喘着气又说。

无人作答。

「在家里可是风靡一时呢。」

无人作答。

「就让我教教你们吧。「您」不肯试试吗，圣人先生（Herr Sage）？」

她走到他跟前，对他行了个小小的屈膝礼。她一向这样称呼他，全然没有察觉——旁人却个个看得明明白白——他对此心怀芥蒂。

===Original===
“Oh come, put away that tiresome old book,” she went on gaily, as he did not move; “I am certain it is only some dry agricultural work that you just nod over. Dancing is much better for you.” Irais and I looked at one another quite frightened. I am sure we both turned pale when the unhappy girl actually laid hold forcibly of his book, and, with a playful little shriek, ran away with it into the next room, hugging it to her bosom and looking back roguishly over her shoulder at him as she ran. There was an awful pause. We hardly dared raise our eyes. Then the Man of Wrath got up slowly, knocked the ashes off the end of his cigar, looked at his watch, and went out at the opposite door into his own rooms, where he stayed for the rest of the evening. She has never, I must say, been skittish since.

===Chinese===
「哦来嘛，把那本讨厌的旧书收起来吧，」见他不动，她又欢快地说下去；「我敢说那不过是本干巴巴的农学著作，您看着看着就打盹的。跳舞对您有益得多。」我和伊蕾斯面面相觑，吓得不轻。当那不幸的姑娘当真一把夺过他的书、顽笑地尖叫一声、抱着它跑进隔壁房间、边跑边回头淘气地瞟他时，我敢说我们俩的脸都白了。接着是一阵可怕的静默。我们几乎不敢抬眼。然后，愤怒之人缓缓起身，磕了磕雪茄烟头的灰，看了看表，从对面那扇门出去，进了他自己的房间，那个晚上再没出来。我必须说一句，打那以后，她再没欢腾过。

===Original===
“I hope you are listening, Miss Minora,” said Irais, “because this sort of conversation is likely to do you good.”

“I always listen when people talk sensibly,” replied Minora, stirring her grog.

Irais glanced at her with slightly doubtful eyebrows. “Do you agree with our hostess’s description of women?” she asked after a pause.

“As nobodies? No, of course I do not.”

“Yet she is right. In the eye of the law we are literally nobodies in our country. Did you know that women are forbidden to go to political meetings here?”

“Really?” Out came the notebook.

“The law expressly forbids the attendance at such meetings of women, children, and idiots.”

===Chinese===
「希望你听着呢，米诺拉小姐，」伊蕾斯说，「这类谈话兴许对你有好处。」

「人们讲道理的时候，我总归是听的。」米诺拉答道，搅着她的那杯格罗格热酒（grog）。

伊蕾斯瞟了她一眼，眉毛微微存疑。「你同意我们女主人对女人的描述吗？」她顿了顿，问道。

「说是无足轻重之辈？不，我当然不同意。」

「可她说得对。在我们国家，在法律眼里，我们实实在在是无足轻重之辈。你知道这里禁止妇女参加政治集会吗？」

「真的吗？」笔记本应声掏了出来。

「法律明文禁止妇女、儿童和白痴出席此类集会。」

===Original===
“Children and idiots—I understand that,” said Minora; “but women—and classed with children and idiots?”

“Classed with children and idiots,” repeated Irais, gravely nodding her head. “Did you know that the law forbids females of any age to ride on the top of omnibuses or tramcars?”

“Not really?”

“Do you know why?”

“I can’t imagine.”

“Because in going up and down the stairs those inside might perhaps catch a glimpse of the stocking covering their ankles.”

“But what—”

“Did you know that the morals of the German public are in such a shaky condition that a glimpse of that sort would be fatal to them?”

“But I don’t see how a stocking—”

“With stripes round it,” said Irais.

“And darns in it,” I added.

“—could possibly be pernicious?”

“‘The Pernicious Stocking; or, Thoughts on the Ethics of Petticoats,’” said Irais. “Put that down as the name of your next book on Germany.”

“I never know,” complained Minora, letting her notebook fall, “whether you are in earnest or not.”

“Don’t you?” said Irais sweetly.

===Chinese===
「儿童和白痴——这个我明白，」米诺拉说，「可女人——居然同儿童和白痴归作一类？」

「同儿童和白痴归作一类。」伊蕾斯重复道，庄重地点头。「你知道法律禁止任何年龄的女性乘坐公共马车或有轨电车的顶层吗？」

「不会吧？」

「知道为什么吗？」

「我想象不出。」

「因为上下楼梯的时候，车厢里的人兴许会瞥见她们罩着脚踝的长袜。」

「可是这——」

「你知道德国公众的道德正处在那样摇摇欲坠的境地，这么瞥上一眼就足以致命吗？」

「可我不明白一只长袜——」

「还是带着一圈圈条纹的呢。」伊蕾斯说。

「还打着补丁的呢。」我补充。

「——怎么就能闯出祸来？」

「《祸害长袜——或曰衬裙伦理随想》，」伊蕾斯说。「就把它记下来，当你下一本写德国的书的书名。」

「我永远搞不清，」米诺拉抱怨道，任笔记本垂落，「你们到底是认真还是不认真。」

「搞不清吗？」伊蕾斯甜甜地说。
===Original===
“Is it true,” appealed Minora to the Man of Wrath, busy with his lemons in the background, “that your law classes women with children and idiots?”

“Certainly,” he answered promptly, “and a very proper classification, too.”

We all looked blank. “That’s rude,” said I at last.

“Truth is always rude, my dear,” he replied complacently. Then he added, “If I were commissioned to draw up a new legal code, and had previously enjoyed the privilege, as I have been doing lately, of listening to the conversation of you three young ladies, I should make precisely the same classification.”

Even Minora was incensed at this.

“You are telling us in the most unvarnished manner that we are idiots,” said Irais.

“Idiots? No, no, by no means. But children—nice little agreeable children. I very much like to hear you talk together. It is all so young and fresh what you think and what you believe, and not of the least consequence to anyone.”

“Not of the least consequence?” cried Minora. “What we believe is of very great consequence indeed to us.”

“Are you jeering at our beliefs?” inquired Irais sternly.

===Chinese===
「是真的吗，」米诺拉向愤怒之人求证，他正在后头忙着对付柠檬，「你们的法律把女人同儿童和白痴归作一类？」

「千真万确，」他应声而答，「而且是再恰当不过的分类。」

我们全都目瞪口呆。「太无礼了。」我终于说。

「真理总是无礼的，亲爱的。」他洋洋自得地答道。接着又说：「倘若我受命起草一部新法典，而又先享有过那份荣幸——近来我一直在享——听你们三位年轻女士交谈，我订出的会是分毫不差同一种分类。」

连米诺拉都被这话说恼了。

「你在用最不加粉饰的方式告诉我们，我们是白痴。」伊蕾斯说。

「白痴？不，不，绝无此意。是儿童——可人又随和的小孩童。我非常爱听你们一起聊天。你们想的、信的都那么年轻新鲜，而且对任何人都无足轻重。」

「无足轻重？」米诺拉叫道。「我们所信的东西，对我们自己实在要紧得很。」

「你是在讥笑我们的信仰吗？」伊蕾斯严厉地质问。

===Original===
“Not for worlds. I would not on any account disturb or change your pretty little beliefs. It is your chief charm that you always believe everything. How desperate would our case be if young ladies only believed facts, and never accepted another person’s assurance, but preferred the evidence of their own eyes! They would have no illusions, and a woman without illusions is the dreariest and most difficult thing to manage possible.”

“Thing?” protested Irais.

The Man of Wrath, usually so silent, makes up for it from time to time by holding forth at unnecessary length. He took up his stand now with his back to the fire, and a glass of *Glühwein* in his hand. Minora had hardly heard his voice before, so quiet had he been since she came, and sat with her pencil raised, ready to fix forever the wisdom that should flow from his lips.

===Chinese===
「万万不敢。我无论如何不肯搅扰或改变你们那些漂亮的小信仰。你们什么都信，这正是你们主要的魅力。要是年轻女士们只肯相信事实，从不接受旁人的保证，偏偏信赖自己眼睛的证据，我们的境况可就绝望透顶了！那样她们便没有幻梦，而一个没有幻梦的女人，是天底下最阴沉、最难对付的东西。」

「东西？」伊蕾斯抗议。

愤怒之人平日那样寡言，隔三差五便要用一篇不必要的长篇大论找补回来。此刻他背炉而立，手里端着一杯热红酒（Glühwein）。米诺拉来后他一直那么安静，她几乎没听他开过口，此刻便坐在那儿举着铅笔，随时准备把他唇间将涌出的智慧永远固定下来。

===Original===
“What would become of poetry if women became so sensible that they turned a deaf ear to the poetic platitudes of love? That love does indulge in platitudes I suppose you will admit.” He looked at Irais.

“Yes, they all say exactly the same thing,” she acknowledged.

“Who could murmur pretty speeches on the beauty of a common sacrifice, if the listener’s want of imagination was such as to enable her only to distinguish one victim in the picture, and that one herself?”

Minora took that down word for word—much good may it do her.

===Chinese===
「倘若女人变得那般明白事理，对爱情那些诗意的陈词滥调充耳不闻，诗还落得个什么下场？爱情尽说陈词滥调，这一点我想你们是承认的。」他望着伊蕾斯。

「是的，它们翻来覆去说的全是同一套。」她承认。

「倘若听者缺乏想象到那步田地，以至于只认得出画中一个牺牲者，而那牺牲者正是她自己，那还有谁能对着『共同牺牲之美』轻声诵出漂亮的颂词呢？」

米诺拉把这句话一字不落记了下来——但愿它对她多多益善地有用。

===Original===
“Who would be brave enough to affirm that if refused he will die, if his assurances merely elicit a recommendation to diet himself, and take plenty of outdoor exercise? Women are responsible for such lies, because they believe them. Their amazing vanity makes them swallow flattery so gross that it is an insult, and men will always be ready to tell the precise number of lies that a woman is ready to listen to. Who indulges more recklessly in glowing exaggerations than the lover who hopes, and has not yet obtained? He will, like the nightingale, sing with unceasing modulations, display all his talent, untiringly repeat his sweetest notes, until he has what he wants, when his song, like the nightingale’s, immediately ceases, never again to be heard.”

“Take that down,” murmured Irais aside to Minora—unnecessary advice, for her pencil was scribbling as fast as it could.

===Chinese===
「谁还敢横下心宣称，遭到拒绝自己便会死，倘若他信誓旦旦换来的，只是一句劝他节食、多做户外运动的建议？这样的谎言要由女人负责，因为她们信。她们那惊人的虚荣使她们吞下露骨得近于侮辱的奉承，而男人永远乐于照着一个女人肯听的数量，不多不少地撒谎。还有谁比那个怀着希望而尚未得手的恋人更无所顾忌地沉溺于炽烈的夸张？他会像夜莺一样，变着调子唱个不停，卖弄全部的才艺，不厌其烦地重复他最甜美的音符，直到得到想要的东西，那时他的歌便同夜莺的一样戛然而止，从此杳无声息。」

「把这段记下来。」伊蕾斯侧身对米诺拉低声说——这忠告纯属多余，她的铅笔早已拼尽全力地疾书。

===Original===
“A woman’s vanity is so immeasurable that, after having had ninety-nine object-lessons in the difference between promise and performance and the emptiness of pretty speeches, the beginning of the hundredth will find her lending the same willing and enchanted ear to the eloquence of flattery as she did on the occasion of the first. What can the exhortations of the strong-minded sister, who has never had these experiences, do for such a woman? It is useless to tell her she is man’s victim, that she is his plaything, that she is cheated, downtrodden, kept under, laughed at, shabbily treated in every way—that is not a true statement of the case. She is simply the victim of her own vanity, and against that, against the belief in her own fascinations, against the very part of herself that gives all the colour to her life, who shall expect a woman to take up arms?”

“Are you so vain, Elizabeth?” inquired Irais with a shocked face, “and had you lent a willing ear to the blandishments of ninety-nine before you reached your final destiny?”

“I am one of the sensible ones, I suppose,” I replied, “for nobody ever wanted me to listen to blandishments.”

Minora sighed.

===Chinese===
「女人的虚荣无涯无际，九十九回现身说法的教训——许诺与兑现的落差、漂亮话的空洞——领教过了，第一百回开场时，她照旧会像第一回那样，如痴如醉、满心欢喜地把耳朵凑向奉承的雄辩。那些从未有过此类经历的意志刚强的姐妹，她们的劝诫对这样的女人又有何用？告诉她她是男人的牺牲品、是他的玩物、她受了骗、被踩在脚下、遭辖制、被嘲笑、样样受亏待——都是白费，这不是对实情的如实陈述。她不过是自己虚荣的牺牲品，要她对抗虚荣、对抗她对自身魅力的笃信、对抗她自己身上那为她的生命着上全部色彩的部分，谁敢指望一个女人提起武器？」

「你也这么虚荣吗，伊丽莎白？」伊蕾斯一脸震惊地问，「你抵达最终归宿之前，也把耳朵借给过九十九个人的甜言蜜语吗？」

「我想我属于明白事理的那一类，」我答道，「因为压根儿没有人要我听过什么甜言蜜语。」

米诺拉叹了口气。
===Original===
“I like to hear you talk together about the position of women,” he went on, “and wonder when you will realise that they hold exactly the position they are fitted for. As soon as they are fit to occupy a better, no power on earth will be able to keep them out of it. Meanwhile, let me warn you that, as things now are, only strong-minded women wish to see you the equals of men, and the strong-minded are invariably plain. The pretty ones would rather see men their slaves than their equals.”

“You know,” said Irais, frowning, “that I consider myself strong-minded.”

“And never rise till lunchtime?”

Irais blushed. Although I don’t approve of such conduct, it is very convenient in more ways than one; I get through my housekeeping undisturbed, and whenever she is disposed to lecture me, I begin about this habit of hers. Her conscience must be terribly stricken on the point, for she is by no means as a rule given to meekness.

===Chinese===
「我爱听你们一起谈论妇女的地位，」他接着说，一面纳闷你们几时才会明白，她们占据的恰恰是与她们相称的地位。一旦她们配得上更好的，世上再没有力量拦得住。不过且让我提醒你们：照眼下的情形，只有意志刚强的女人才愿意看到你们与男人平等，而意志刚强的女人一概其貌不扬。漂亮的女人们宁愿男人做她们的奴隶，也不愿他们做平辈。」

「你知道，」伊蕾斯皱着眉说，「我自认为是意志刚强的。」

「却从来到午饭时分才起床？」

伊蕾斯脸红了。这种行径我虽不赞成，却在不止一方面非常方便；我可以不受打扰地料理完家务，而每当她想教训我，我就提起她这个习惯。她在这一点上良心必定负疚深重，因为她平日绝少肯低头服软。

===Original===
“A woman without vanity would be unattackable,” resumed the Man of Wrath. “When a girl enters that downward path that leads to ruin, she is led solely by her own vanity; for in these days of policemen no young woman can be forced against her will from the path of virtue, and the cries of the injured are never heard until the destroyer begins to express his penitence for having destroyed. If his passion could remain at white-heat and he could continue to feed her ear with the protestations she loves, no principles of piety or virtue would disturb the happiness of his companion; for a mournful experience teaches that piety begins only where passion ends, and that principles are strongest where temptations are most rare.”

“But what has all this to do with us?” I inquired severely.

“You were displeased at our law classing you as it does, and I merely wish to justify it,” he answered. “Creatures who habitually say *yes* to everything a man proposes, when no one can oblige them to say it, and when it is so often fatal, are plainly not responsible beings.”

“I shall never say it to you again, my dear man,” I said.

===Chinese===
「没有虚荣的女人是无懈可击的。」愤怒之人重新开讲。「少女走上那条通向毁灭的下坡路时，领路的全是她自己的虚荣；因为在这满街警察的年代，没有哪个年轻女子能被违背意愿地架离美德之路，而且受害者的哭喊从来无人听见，直到那毁灭者开始为他的毁灭表示悔意。倘若他的激情能始终白热，能继续用她爱听的那些誓言喂养她的耳朵，那么任何虔敬或贞洁的原则都搅扰不了他女伴的幸福；因为惨痛的经验教导我们：虔敬始于激情止步之处，而原则在诱惑最稀少的地方最强大。」

「可这些同我们有什么相干？」我严厉地质问。

「你们对我们法律如此归类心怀不满，我不过想证明它有理。」他答道。「一种造物，对男人提出的一切照例都说『是』，谁也没强迫她们说，而这『是』又往往致命，这样的造物明明白白算不得有责任能力的人。」

「我再也不对你说这个字了，亲爱的男人。」我说。

===Original===
“And not only *that* fatal weakness,” he continued, “but what is there, candidly, to distinguish you from children? You are older, but not wiser—really not so wise, for with years you lose the common sense you had as children. Have you ever heard a group of women talking reasonably together?”

“Yes—we do!” Irais and I cried in a breath.

“It has interested me,” went on the Man of Wrath, “in my idle moments, to listen to their talk. It amused me to hear the malicious little stories they told of their best friends who were absent, to note the spiteful little digs they gave their best friends who were present, to watch the utter incredulity with which they listened to the tale of some other woman’s conquests, the radiant good faith they displayed in connection with their own, the instant collapse into boredom, if some topic of so-called general interest, by some extraordinary chance, were introduced.”

“You must have belonged to a particularly nice set,” remarked Irais.

“And as for politics,” he said, “I have never heard them mentioned among women.”

“Children and idiots are not interested in such things,” I said.

“And we are much too frightened of being put in prison,” said Irais.

===Chinese===
「还不止「那」一个致命弱点，」他继续说，「坦白讲，你们跟儿童又有什么两样？你们年长些，却不更聪明——实在说还不如小时候聪明，因为随着年岁增长，你们把做小姑娘时那点常识丢掉了。你们可曾听过一群女人通情达理地交谈？」

「听过——我们现在就在这样谈！」我和伊蕾斯异口同声地喊。

「闲来无事听她们聊天，倒也让我觉得有趣。」愤怒之人继续说。「听她们讲缺席挚友的恶意小故事，让我觉得好玩；留意她们给在场挚友使的恶毒小刺；观察她们听别的女人的风流战绩时那份彻底的不信任、提起自己战绩时那份容光焕发的笃信；还有万一——纯属巧合——有人提起某个所谓大众关心的话题，她们如何顷刻瘫软成无聊。」

「你结交的必定是个格外和美的圈子。」伊蕾斯评论道。

「至于政治，」他说，「我从没听见女人们谈论过。」

「儿童和白痴对这种事不感兴趣。」我说。

「再说我们怕得要命，怕给关进监狱去。」伊蕾斯说。

===Original===
“In prison?” echoed Minora.

“Don’t you know,” said Irais, turning to her, “that if you talk about such things here you run a great risk of being imprisoned?”

“But why?”

“But why? Because, though you yourself may have meant nothing but what was innocent, your words may have suggested something less innocent to the evil minds of your hearers; and then the law steps in, and calls it *dolus eventualis*, and everybody says how dreadful, and off you go to prison and are punished as you deserve to be.”

Minora looked mystified.

“That is not, however, your real reason for not discussing them,” said the Man of Wrath; “they simply do not interest you. Or it may be, that you do not consider your female friends’ opinions worth listening to, for you certainly display an astonishing thirst for information when male politicians are present. I have seen a pretty young woman, hardly in her twenties, sitting a whole evening drinking in the doubtful wisdom of an elderly political star, with every appearance of eager interest. He was a bimetallic star, and was giving her whole pamphletsful of information.”

===Chinese===
「进监狱？」米诺拉应声问。

「你不知道吗，」伊蕾斯转向她说，「在这里谈论这种事，是要冒坐牢的大风险的？」

「可为什么？」

「为什么？因为尽管你自己也许一腔清白、别无他意，你的话却可能在听者邪恶的心里勾起些不太清白的东西；于是法律介入，管它叫间接故意（dolus eventualis），人人惊呼可怕极了，于是你被投进监狱，罪有应得地领受惩罚。」

米诺拉一脸困惑。

「不过，那并不是你们不谈政治的真正原因，」愤怒之人说，「它们根本引不起你们的兴趣。要不然，就是你们认为女性朋友们的见解不值得听，因为一旦在场的政治家是男的，你们分明显示出惊人的求知渴求。我见过一个漂亮的年轻女子，年纪还未满二十，整晚坐在那里，带着全然热切的神情，痛饮一位年迈政治明星那可疑的智慧。他是颗复本位制的星，正成册成册地向她倾泻小册子分量的信息。」

===Original===
“She wanted to make up to him for some reason,” said Irais, “and got him to explain his hobby to her, and he was silly enough to be taken in. Now which was the sillier in that case?”

She threw herself back in her chair and looked up defiantly, beating her foot impatiently on the carpet.

“She wanted to be thought clever,” said the Man of Wrath. “What puzzled me,” he went on musingly, “was that she went away apparently as serene and happy as when she came. The explanation of the principles of bimetallism produce, as a rule, a contrary effect.”

“Why, she hadn’t been listening,” cried Irais, “and your simple star had been making a fine goose of himself the whole evening.

> “Prattle, prattle, simple star,
 
 Bimetallic, *wunderbar*.
 Though you’re given to describe
 Woman as a *dummes Weib*.
 You yourself are sillier far,
 Prattling, bimetallic star!”

===Chinese===
「她是出于什么缘故想讨好他，」伊蕾斯说，「便哄他讲解他的癖好，而他傻乎乎地上了钩。那么请问，这一场里哪一个更傻？」

她猛地靠回椅背，抬眼挑衅地望着，一只脚不耐烦地敲着地毯。

「她是想让人夸她聪明。」愤怒之人说。「让我纳闷的是，」他若有所思地接着说，「她离开时竟同来时一样安详快活。复本位制原理的讲解，照例会产生恰恰相反的效果。」

「嗨，她根本没在听，」伊蕾斯喊道，「而你这颗单纯的星，整个晚上都在把自己扮成一只漂亮的大鹅。

> 「絮叨，絮叨，单纯星，
 复本位制，*wunderbar*（妙不可言）。
 你惯爱把女人描绘成
 *dummes Weib*（蠢婆娘）。
 可你自己蠢得远甚，
 絮絮叨叨，复本位之星！」
===Original===
“No doubt she had understood very little,” said the Man of Wrath, taking no notice of this effusion.

“And no doubt the gentleman hadn’t understood much either.” Irais was plainly irritated.

“Your opinion of woman,” said Minora in a very small voice, “is not a high one. But, in the sick chamber, I suppose you agree that no one could take her place?”

“If you are thinking of hospital-nurses,” I said, “I must tell you that I believe he married chiefly that he might have a wife instead of a strange woman to nurse him when he is sick.”

“But,” said Minora, bewildered at the way her illusions were being knocked about, “the sickroom is surely the very place of all others in which a woman’s gentleness and tact are most valuable.”

===Chinese===
「毫无疑问，她听懂的很少。」愤怒之人说，对这段即兴诗未加理会。

「毫无疑问，那位先生懂得也不多。」伊蕾斯分明恼了。

「你对女人的看法，」米诺拉声音很小地说，「可算不上高。可是在病房里，我猜你总该同意，谁也替代不了她的位置吧？」

「你要是想的是医院护士，」我说，「那我必须告诉你，我相信他结婚，主要是为了生病时守在床边的是妻子，而不是一个陌生女人。」

「可是，」米诺拉说，眼见自己的幻梦被撞得东倒西歪，一脸茫然，「病房当然正是那独一无二的所在，女人的温柔与机变在那里最显宝贵。」

===Original===
“Gentleness and tact?” repeated the Man of Wrath. “I have never met those qualities in the professional nurse. According to my experience, she is a disagreeable person who finds in private nursing exquisite opportunities for asserting her superiority over ordinary and prostrate mankind. I know of no more humiliating position for a man than to be in bed having his feverish brow soothed by a sprucely-dressed strange woman, bristling with starch and spotlessness. He would give half his income for his clothes, and probably the other half if she would leave him alone, and go away altogether. He feels her superiority through every pore; he never before realised how absolutely inferior he is; he is abjectly polite, and contemptibly conciliatory; if a friend comes to see him, he eagerly praises her in case she should be listening behind the screen; he cannot call his soul his own, and, what is far more intolerable, neither is he sure that his body really belongs to him; he has read of ministering angels and the light touch of a woman’s hand, but the day on which he can ring for his servant and put on his socks in private fills him with the same sort of wildness of joy that he felt as a homesick schoolboy at the end of his first term.”

Minora was silent. Irais’s foot was livelier than ever. The Man of Wrath stood smiling blandly down upon us. You can’t argue with a person so utterly convinced of his infallibility that he won’t even get angry with you; so we sat round and said nothing.

===Chinese===
「温柔和机变？」愤怒之人重复道。「我在职业护士身上从未遇到过这两种品质。照我的经验，她是个惹人厌的人物，把私人看护当作宣示自己优于平凡而卧床的人类的绝佳机会。我知道对一个男人来说，没有比这更屈辱的处境了：躺在床上，滚烫的额头由一个衣冠楚楚的陌生女人来抚慰，浑身是浆得硬挺的挺括和一尘不染。他情愿拿一半收入换回他的衣服，她若肯彻底走开、不再打搅，另外那一半多半也肯搭上。他每一个毛孔都感到她的优越；他有生以来从未意识到自己是如此彻底地低人一等；他谦卑地客气，没出息地讨好；若有朋友来探望，他便急切地夸她，生怕她正在屏风后听着；他连自己的灵魂都做不得主，而更难忍受的是，他连自己的身体究竟还归不归自己、都不能确信；他读到过守护天使，读到过女人之手的轻抚，可是当他终于能够摇铃唤仆人、私下里自己穿上袜子的那一天，他心里涌起的狂喜，同他做想家的学童、第一学期结束那天一模一样。」

米诺拉不作声了。伊蕾斯的脚敲得比先前更欢。愤怒之人站在那儿，和颜悦色地俯视着我们。同这样的人争辩不得——他对自己的万无一失信得那样彻底，连对你发火都不屑；于是我们围坐一圈，一言不发。

===Original===
“If,” he went on, addressing Irais, who looked rebellious, “you doubt the truth of my remarks, and still cling to the old poetic notion of noble, self-sacrificing women tenderly helping the patient over the rough places on the road to death or recovery, let me beg you to try for yourself, next time anyone in your house is ill, whether the actual fact in any way corresponds to the picturesque belief. The angel who is to alleviate our sufferings comes in such a questionable shape, that to the unimaginative she appears merely as an extremely self-confident young woman, wisely concerned first of all in securing her personal comfort, much given to complaints about her food and to helplessness where she should be helpful, possessing an extraordinary capacity for fancying herself slighted, or not regarded as the superior being she knows herself to be, morbidly anxious lest the servants should, by some mistake, treat her with offensive cordiality, pettish if the patient gives more trouble than she had expected, intensely injured and disagreeable if he is made so courageous by his wretchedness as to wake her during the night—an act of desperation of which I was guilty once, and once only. Oh, these good women! What sane man wants to have to do with angels? And especially do we object to having them about us when we are sick and sorry, when we feel in every fibre what poor things we are, and when all our fortitude is needed to enable us to bear our temporary inferiority patiently, without being forced besides to assume an attitude of eager and grovelling politeness towards the angel in the house.”

There was a pause.

“I didn’t know you could talk so much, Sage,” said Irais at length.

===Chinese===
「倘若，」他接着说，对象是看上去要造反的伊蕾斯，「你怀疑我这番话的真实性，仍然抱着那种古老的诗意观念，以为女人高贵无私、温柔地搀扶病人走过通向死亡或康复的崎岖路，那么请你下次家里有人生病时亲自试试，看实际情形是否同这幅如画的信念有半点相符。那位前来减轻我们苦难的天使，降临的模样如此可疑，在缺乏想象力的人眼里，她无非是个自信过人的年轻女人，头一件事是精明地先安顿好自己的舒适，动辄抱怨伙食，在恰恰该出力的地方束手无策，有着一种非凡的本领，总能觉得自己受了怠慢、没被当作她自知的那种优越生灵来对待，病态地担心仆人们万一弄错、待她亲热得冒犯了她，病人添的麻烦若超出她的预期，她便使性子，他若被病痛逼出胆量、竟敢在夜里叫醒她——那可是我犯过一次、也仅仅一次的绝望之举——她便委屈到极点、难看到极点。哦，这些好女人们！哪个心智健全的男人想同天使打交道？尤其是当我们又病又愁、浑身上下每一根纤维都感到自己是何等可怜的东西、需要拿出全部坚忍才能耐住一时低人一等的时候，却又偏偏被迫对屋中的天使摆出一副急切而卑躬的客气——那就尤其要不得。」

一阵静默。

「我原来不知道你能说这么多话，圣人。」伊蕾斯终于开口。

===Original===
“What would you have women do, then?” asked Minora meekly. Irais began to beat her foot up and down again—what did it matter what Men of Wrath would have us do? “There are not,” continued Minora, blushing, “husbands enough for everyone, and the rest must do something.”

“Certainly,” replied the oracle. “Study the art of pleasing by dress and manner as long as you are of an age to interest us, and above all, let all women, pretty and plain, married and single, study the art of cookery. If you are an artist in the kitchen you will always be esteemed.”

I sat very still. Every German woman, even the wayward Irais, has learned to cook; I seem to have been the only one who was naughty and wouldn’t.

===Chinese===
「那么依你看，女人该做什么呢？」米诺拉温顺地问。伊蕾斯又开始上下踩她的脚——愤怒之人们要我们做什么，干卿何事？「丈夫，」米诺拉红着脸继续说，「不够每个女人分的，剩下的人总得做点什么。」

「那是自然。」神谕答道。「趁着你们还处在能让我们发生兴趣的年纪，用心研究以服饰和仪态取悦的艺术；而最重要的，是让所有的女人，漂亮的和平凡的，已婚的和单身的，都研究烹饪之艺。你若是厨房里的艺术家，永远受人敬重。」

我端坐不动。每一个德国女人，连任性的伊蕾斯都在内，都学过烹饪；不肯学的不乖的，似乎只有我一个。

===Original===
“Only be careful,” he went on, “in studying both arts, never to forget the great truth that dinner precedes blandishments and not blandishments dinner. A man must be made comfortable before he will make love to you; and though it is true that if you offered him a choice between *Spickgans* and kisses, he would say he would take both, yet he would invariably begin with the *Spickgans*, and allow the kisses to wait.”

At this I got up, and Irais followed my example. “Your cynicism is disgusting,” I said icily.

“You two are always exceptions to anything I may say,” he said, smiling amiably.

He stooped and kissed Irais’s hand. She is inordinately vain of her hands, and says her husband married her for their sake, which I can quite believe. I am glad they are on her and not on Minora, for if Minora had had them I should have been annoyed. Minora’s are bony, with chilly-looking knuckles, ignored nails, and too much wrist. I feel very well disposed towards her when my eye falls on them. She put one forward now, evidently thinking it would be kissed too.

“Did you know,” said Irais, seeing the movement, “that it is the custom here to kiss women’s hands?”

“But only married women’s,” I added, not desiring her to feel out of it, “never young girls’.”

She drew it in again. “It is a pretty custom,” she said with a sigh; and pensively inscribed it in her book.

===Chinese===
「只是要当心，」他接着说，「研究这两门艺术时，切莫忘掉那条伟大的真理：晚餐在甜言蜜语之先，而非甜言蜜语在晚餐之先。必得先让一个男人舒舒服服，他才肯向你求爱；虽说不错，你若让他二选一，烟熏鹅（Spickgans）还是亲吻，他会说两个都要，可他准会先从烟熏鹅下手，让亲吻等着。」

听到这里我站起身，伊蕾斯学了我的样。「你这愤世的腔调叫人恶心。」我冷冷地说。

「你们两位永远是我所说一切之外的例外。」他说，笑得和蔼可亲。

他俯身吻了伊蕾斯的手。她对自己的两只手虚荣得无以复加，说她丈夫是为了它们才娶她的，这话我完全信。幸亏它们长在她身上而不是米诺拉身上，倘若长在米诺拉身上，我就要恼火了。米诺拉的手瘦骨嶙峋，指节看上去冷冰冰，指甲无人照管，手腕又太长。我的目光每落到它们上面，便对她生出无限好感。此刻她伸出一只手来，分明以为它也会得到一吻。

「你知道吗，」伊蕾斯瞅见这个动作，说道，「这里有个风俗，要吻女人的手？」

「不过只吻已婚女人的，」我补上一句，不想让她觉得受了冷落，「从不吻年轻姑娘的。」

她又把手缩了回去。「真是个雅致的风俗。」她叹口气说；然后若有所思地把它记进了她的本子。

## January 15th / 1月15日

===Original===
The bills for my roses and bulbs and other last year’s horticultural indulgences were all on the table when I came down to breakfast this morning. They rather frightened me. Gardening is expensive, I find, when it has to be paid for out of one’s own private pin-money. The Man of Wrath does not in the least want roses, or flowering shrubs, or plantations, or new paths, and therefore, he asks, why should he pay for them? So he does not and I do, and I have to make up for it by not indulging all too riotously in new clothes, which is no doubt very chastening. I certainly prefer buying new rose-trees to new dresses, if I cannot comfortably have both; and I see a time coming when the passion for my garden will have taken such a hold on me that I shall not only entirely cease buying more clothes, but begin to sell those that I already have. The garden is so big that everything has to be bought wholesale; and I fear I shall not be able to go on much longer with only one man and a stork, because the more I plant the more there will be to water in the inevitable drought, and the watering is a serious consideration when it means going backwards and forwards all day long to a pump near the house, with a little water-cart. People living in England, in almost perpetual mildness and moisture, don’t really know what a drought is. If they have some weeks of cloudless weather, it is generally preceded and followed by good rains; but we have perhaps an hour’s shower every week, and then comes a month or six weeks’ drought. The soil is very light, and dries so quickly that, after the heaviest thundershower, I can walk over any of my paths in my thin shoes; and to keep the garden even moderately damp it should pour with rain regularly every day for three hours. My only means of getting water is to go to the pump near the house, or to the little stream that forms my eastern boundary, and the little stream dries up too unless there has been rain, and is at the best of times difficult to get at, having steep banks covered with forget-me-nots. I possess one moist, peaty bit of ground, and that is to be planted with silver birches in imitation of the Hirschwald, and is to be carpeted between the birches with flaming azaleas. All the rest of my soil is sandy—the soil for pines and acacias, but not the soil for roses; yet see what love will do—there are more roses in my garden than any other flower! Next spring the bare places are to be filled with trees that I have ordered: pines behind the delicate acacias, and startling mountain-ashes, oaks, copper-beeches, maples, larches, juniper-trees—was it not Elijah who sat down to rest under a juniper-tree? I have often wondered how he managed to get under it. It is a compact little tree, not more than two to three yards high here, and all closely squeezed up together. Perhaps they grew more aggressively where he was. By the time the babies have grown old and disagreeable it will be very pretty here, and then possibly they won’t like it; and, if they have inherited the Man of Wrath’s indifference to gardens, they will let it run wild and leave it to return to the state in which I found it. Or perhaps their three husbands will refuse to live in it, or to come to such a lonely place at all, and then of course its fate is sealed. My only comfort is that husbands don’t flourish in the desert, and that the three will have to wait a long time before enough are found to go round. Mothers tell me that it is a dreadful business finding one husband; how much more painful then to have to look for three at once!—the babies are so nearly the same age that they only just escaped being twins. But I won’t look. I can imagine nothing more uncomfortable than a son-in-law, and besides, I don’t think a husband is at all a good thing for a girl to have. I shall do my best in the years at my disposal to train them so to love the garden, and outdoor life, and even farming, that, if they have a spark of their mother in them, they will want and ask for nothing better. My hope of success is however exceedingly small, and there is probably a fearful period in store for me when I shall be taken every day during the winter to the distant towns to balls—a poor old mother shivering in broad daylight in her party gown, and being made to start after an early lunch and not getting home till breakfast-time next morning. Indeed, they have already developed an alarming desire to go to “partings” as they call them, the April baby announcing her intention of beginning to do so when she is twelve. “Are *you* twelve, Mummy?” she asked.

===Chinese===
今天早晨我下楼用早餐时，去年买玫瑰、球根和其他种种园艺放纵的账单全都摆在桌上。它们颇叫我心惊。我发现，园艺是要花大钱的，当它必须从自己的私房零用钱里支付时。愤怒之人一点儿也不想要玫瑰，不想要开花灌木，不想要林木，不想要新辟的小径，所以他问，凭什么要他掏钱？于是他不掏，我掏，而我不得不靠克制自己、别太放肆地添置新衣裳来弥补亏空，这无疑很有砥砺心性的功效。倘若不能两样舒舒服服兼得，我当然宁买新玫瑰树，不买新连衣裙；而且我已望见那么一天：对花园的狂热把我攫取得那样紧，我不但要彻底停止添衣，还要开始变卖现有的。花园大得很，样样都得成批购买；我担心，光靠一个人和一只鹳鸟，我撑不了多久了，因为种的越多，那不可避免的旱季里要浇的就越多，而浇水是桩要认真掂量的事——它意味着赶着一辆小水车，一整天来回奔波于屋旁的水泵之间。住在英格兰的人，那儿几乎永远温和湿润，实在不知道旱灾是什么滋味。他们若遇上几个星期无云的天气，前后照例都有充沛的雨水；我们却是也许每星期下一小时的阵雨，然后就是一个月或六个星期的干旱。土质非常轻，干得飞快，最大的一场雷雨过后，我穿着薄底鞋就能走遍园中任何一条小径；要让花园保持哪怕适度的湿润，也得天天按时倾盆下上三个钟头。我取水的唯一办法，是去屋旁的水泵，或者去构成我东面边界的那条小溪；小溪若久无雨水也会干涸，而且即便在最好的时节也难以取水，两岸陡峭，长满了勿忘我。我拥有一小块湿润的泥炭地，那儿将仿照希尔施瓦尔德（Hirschwald，德语「鹿林」）栽上白桦，桦树之间将铺满如火的杜鹃。其余的土全是沙土——那是宜松树和刺槐的土，不是宜玫瑰的土；可是瞧瞧爱的能耐——我园里的玫瑰比任何别的花都多！明年春天，光秃的地方将栽满我订购的树木：娇柔的刺槐后面植上松树，还有令人惊艳的花楸、栎树、紫叶山毛榉、枫树、落叶松、杜松——在杜松树下坐下歇息的，不正是以利亚吗？我常常纳闷他是怎么钻到树底下去的。杜松在这里是一种紧凑的小树，高不过两三码，枝叶紧紧挤作一团。也许在他那个地方，它们长得更舒展些。等到三个宝贝长到又老又不讨人喜欢的时候，这里将出落得非常漂亮，到那时她们很可能偏偏不喜欢；倘若她们继承了愤怒之人对花园的冷漠，她们便会任它荒芜，随它退回我初来时的模样。又或者她们的三位丈夫会拒绝住在这里，或者干脆不肯到这样偏僻的地方来，那它的命运自然就此注定。我唯一的安慰是，丈夫们在荒漠里长不旺，三姐妹得等上好一阵子，才凑得齐人人有份的数目。做母亲的告诉我，找一个丈夫已是天大的难事；那么一次要找三个，岂不更加苦不堪言！——三个宝贝年龄挨得那样近，勉强才算躲过了双胞胎的命运。反正我不去找。我想象不出比女婿更叫人别扭的东西；再说，我也不认为丈夫这种东西对姑娘家有什么好处。在归我支配的这些年里，我要尽力把她们调教得热爱花园、热爱户外生活、甚至热爱农事，这样，只要她们身上有一星半点母亲的血性，便会别无所求，也不想要更好的了。然而我成功的希望极其渺茫，多半还有一个可怕的时期在等着我：冬天里天天被拽到遥远的城镇去赴舞会——一个可怜的老母亲，大白天里穿着礼服发抖，午饭一过就被押着出发，到第二天早饭时分才被领回家。说真的，她们早已显露出一股令人心惊的欲望，想去参加她们口中的「离别会」（partings，把 parties 说岔了），四月宝贝还宣布，她打算满了十二岁就开始去。「妈妈，「你」满十二岁了吗？」她问。

===Original===
The gardener is leaving on the first of April, and I am trying to find another. It is grievous changing so often—in two years I shall have had three—because at each change a great part of my plants and plans necessarily suffers. Seeds get lost, seedlings are not pricked out in time, places already sown are planted with something else, and there is confusion out of doors and despair in my heart. But he was to have married the cook, and the cook saw a ghost and immediately left, and he is going after her as soon as he can, and meanwhile is wasting visibly away. What she saw was doors that are locked opening with a great clatter all by themselves *on the hinge-side*, and then somebody invisible cursed at her. These phenomena now go by the name of “the ghost.” She asked to be allowed to leave at once, as she had never been in a place where there was a ghost before. I suggested that she should try and get used to it; but she thought it would be wasting time, and she looked so ill that I let her go, and the garden has to suffer. I don’t know why it should be given to cooks to see such interesting things and withheld from me, but I have had two others since she left, and they both have seen the ghost. Minora grows very silent as bedtime approaches, and relents towards Irais and myself; and, after having shown us all day how little she approves us, when the bedroom candles are brought she quite begins to cling. She has once or twice anxiously inquired whether Irais is sure she does not object to sleeping alone.

===Chinese===
园丁四月一日要走，我正设法另找一位。换得这样勤实在叫人痛心——两年里我将用过三个——因为每换一次，我的花草和规划必有大半遭殃。种子弄丢了，秧苗没及时移栽，播过种的地方又被种上了别的东西，园子里一团混乱，我心里一片绝望。可他本来是要娶厨娘的，厨娘见了鬼，立马走了，他呢，一逮着机会就要去追她，这些日子眼见得消瘦下去。她所见到的，是锁得好好的门「顺着铰链那一侧」哐啷一声自己开了起来，接着有个看不见的人咒骂她。这些现象如今都以「那个鬼」闻名。她请求即刻离工，说她从没在闹鬼的人家待过。我建议她试着习惯习惯；可她觉得那纯是浪费时间，她那脸色又那样难看，我便放她走了，花园只好受委屈。我不明白，何以这样有趣的事偏偏赐给厨娘们看，却单单瞒着我；反正自她走后我又用过两个厨娘，她们俩都见过那个鬼。米诺拉一到就寝时分便沉默寡言起来，对我和伊蕾斯也软了下来；白天刚刚让我们看够了她是多么不待见我们，卧室蜡烛一端上来，她便相当黏人了。她已不止一次焦虑地打听，伊蕾斯是不是真的不介意一个人睡。

===Original===
“If you are at all nervous, I will come and keep you company,” she said; “I don’t mind at all, I assure you.”

But Irais is not to be taken in by such simple wiles, and has told me she would rather sleep with fifty ghosts than with one Minora.

===Chinese===
「你要是有一丁点儿心慌，我就过来陪你，」她说；「我完全不介意，真的。」

可伊蕾斯才不会上这种小把戏的当，她告诉过我，她宁可同五十个鬼睡，也不愿同一位米诺拉睡。

===Original===
Since Miss Jones was so unexpectedly called away to her parent’s bedside I have seen a good deal of the babies; and it is so nice without a governess that I would put off engaging another for a year or two, if it were not that I should in so doing come within the reach of the arm of the law, which is what every German spends his life in trying to avoid. The April baby will be six next month, and, after her sixth birthday is passed, we are liable at any moment to receive a visit from a school inspector, who will inquire curiously into the state of her education, and, if it is not up to the required standard, all sorts of fearful things might happen to the guilty parents, probably beginning with fines, and going on crescendo to dungeons if, owing to gaps between governesses and difficulties in finding the right one, we persisted in our evil courses. Shades of the prison-house begin to close here upon the growing boy, and prisons compass the Teuton about on every side all through life to such an extent that he has to walk very delicately indeed if he would stay outside them and pay for their maintenance. Cultured individuals do not, as a rule, neglect to teach their offspring to read, and write, and say their prayers, and are apt to resent the intrusion of an examining inspector into their homes; but it does not much matter after all, and I daresay it is very good for us to be worried; indeed, a philosopher of my acquaintance declares that people who are not regularly and properly worried are never any good for anything. In the eye of the law we are all sinners, and every man is held to be guilty until he has proved that he is innocent.

===Chinese===
自从琼斯小姐那样突然地被召回到她父亲病榻前，我同三个宝贝相处的时间多了许多；没有家庭教师的日子实在惬意，我真愿把再聘一位的事拖上一两年，若非这样一来我便落入了法律之臂的势力范围——那是每个德国人终其一生都在设法躲避的东西。四月宝贝下个月满六岁，过了六岁生日，我们随时可能接待一位督学的来访，他会饶有兴味地查问她受教育的状况；倘若达不到规定标准，有罪的父母便可能遭逢种种可怕之事，多半从罚款开始，渐次升级直至地牢——倘若我们因为两任家庭教师之间的空档、再加上难觅合适人选，仍执迷不悟、怙恶不悛的话。「牢狱之影开始向成长中的孩子合拢」（华兹华斯《不朽颂》中的句子），而监狱终其一生从四面八方围困着条顿人，围得那样严实，他若想置身狱外——还得掏钱供养它们——当真得步步踩得小心。有教养的人照例不会疏于教子女读书、写字、做祷告，因而往往恼恨督学闯进家门；不过说到底也无大碍，而且我敢说，有人来烦一烦对我们大有好处；真的，我认识的一位哲学家断言，凡不能按时按量被烦恼折磨的人，什么事也成不了。在法律眼里我们个个是罪人，人人都被认定有罪，直到他证明自己清白为止。

===Original===
Minora has seen so much of the babies that, after vainly trying to get out of their way for several days, she thought it better to resign herself, and make the best of it by regarding them as copy, and using them to fill a chapter in her book. So she took to dogging their footsteps wherever they went, attended their uprisings and their lyings down, engaged them, if she could, in intelligent conversation, went with them into the garden to study their ways when they were sleighing, drawn by a big dog, and generally made their lives a burden to them. This went on for three days, and then she settled down to write the result with the Man of Wrath’s typewriter, borrowed whenever her notes for any chapter have reached the state of ripeness necessary for the process she describes as “throwing into form.” She writes everything with a typewriter, even her private letters.

===Chinese===
米诺拉同三个宝贝打足了照面，徒劳地躲了几天之后，觉得不如认命，把他们当作素材善加利用，拿他们去填她书里的一章。于是她到处尾随他们的脚步，观摩他们的起床和就寝，逮着机会就同他们展开益智的谈话，跟他们到花园里去考察他们让大狗拉着雪橇玩时的习性，总而言之，把他们的日子搅成了苦役。这样进行了三天，然后她安顿下来，用愤怒之人的打字机把成果写出来——每逢某一章的笔记熟烂到她所谓「整理成形」的工序所必需的火候，这台打字机就被借了来。她什么都用打字机写，连私人信件也是。

===Original===
“Don’t forget to put in something about a mother’s knee,” said Irais; “you can’t write effectively about children without that.”

“Oh, of course I shall mention that,” replied Minora.

“And pink toes,” I added. “There are always toes, and they are never anything but pink.”

“I have that somewhere,” said Minora, turning over her notes.

===Chinese===
「别忘了添上几笔母亲的膝头，」伊蕾斯说；「缺了它，写孩子就写不出效果。」

「哦，那个我当然会提。」米诺拉答道。

「还有粉红的脚趾。」我补充。「脚趾总是有的，而且从来只有粉红的一种。」

「那个我哪儿记着一条。」米诺拉说，一面翻她的笔记。

===Original===
“But, after all, babies are not a German speciality,” said Irais, “and I don’t quite see why you should bring them into a book of German travels. Elizabeth’s babies have each got the fashionable number of arms and legs, and are exactly the same as English ones.”

“Oh, but they can’t be *just* the same, you know,” said Minora, looking worried. “It must make a difference living here in this place, and eating such odd things, and never having a doctor, and never being ill. Children who have never had measles and those things can’t be quite the same as other children; it must all be in their systems and can’t get out for some reason or other. And a child brought up on chicken and rice-pudding must be different to a child that eats *Spickgans* and liver sausages. And they *are* different; I can’t tell in what way, but they certainly are; and I think if I steadily describe them from the materials I have collected the last three days, I may perhaps hit on the points of difference.”

===Chinese===
「可是说到底，娃娃算不得德国的特产，」伊蕾斯说，「我不大明白你为什么要把他们写进一本德国游记。伊丽莎白的娃娃们，胳膊腿都长着时兴的数目，同英国娃娃一模一样。」

「哦，可他们总不致「一模一样」吧，你知道，」米诺拉说，面有忧色。「住在这样一个地方，吃着这样古怪的东西，从来没请过医生，从来没生过病，这必定造成差异。从没出过麻疹之类毛病的儿童，不可能同别的儿童完全一样；那些东西一定都窝在他们体内，因为什么缘故出不来。吃鸡肉和米饭布丁长大的孩子，必定不同于吃烟熏鹅（Spickgans）和肝肠的孩子。他们「确实」不同；我说不出差在哪方面，可他们肯定不同；我想，只要我照着这三天搜集的材料不辍地描写他们，或许就能撞上那些差异之点。」

===Original===
“Why bother about points of difference?” asked Irais. “I should write some little thing, bringing in the usual parts of the picture, such as knees and toes, and make it mildly pathetic.”

“But it is by no means an easy thing for me to do,” said Minora plaintively; “I have so little experience of children.”

“Then why write it at all?” asked that sensible person Elizabeth.

“I have as little experience as you,” said Irais, “because I have no children; but if you don’t yearn after startling originality, nothing is easier than to write bits about them. I believe I could do a dozen in an hour.”

She sat down at the writing-table, took up an old letter, and scribbled for about five minutes. “There,” she said, throwing it to Minora, “you may have it—pink toes and all complete.”

Minora put on her eyeglasses and read aloud:

===Chinese===
「何必费劲找什么差异之点？」伊蕾斯问。「换了我，就写篇小东西，把画面惯常的部件，诸如膝头和脚趾，一一带到，再添几分淡淡的哀婉。」

「可这对我绝非易事，」米诺拉哀声说；「我对孩子的经验实在太少。」

「那干脆写它做什么？」那位明白人伊丽莎白问道。

「我的经验跟你一样少，」伊蕾斯说，「因为我没有孩子；可只要你不渴求惊人的独创性，写几段娃娃的文字再容易不过。我相信我一小时能写一打。」

她在写字台前坐下，抓起一封旧信，涂了约莫五分钟。「喏，」她把它抛给米诺拉，「送你了——粉红脚趾，一应俱全。」

米诺拉戴上夹鼻眼镜，朗读起来：

===Original===
> “When my baby shuts her eyes and sings her hymns at bedtime my stale and battered soul is filled with awe. All sorts of vague memories crowd into my mind—memories of my own mother and myself—how many years ago!—of the sweet helplessness of being gathered up half asleep in her arms, and undressed, and put in my cot, without being wakened; of the angels I believed in; of little children coming straight from heaven, and still being surrounded, so long as they were good, by the shadow of white wings—all the dear poetic nonsense learned, just as my baby is learning it, at her mother’s knee. She has not an idea of the beauty of the charming things she is told, and stares wide-eyed, with heavenly eyes, while her mother talks of the heaven she has so lately come from, and is relieved and comforted by the interrupting bread and milk. At two years old she does not understand angels, and does understand bread and milk; at five she has vague notions about them, and prefers bread and milk; at ten both bread and milk and angels have been left behind in the nursery, and she has already found out that they are luxuries not necessary to her everyday life. In later years she may be disinclined to accept truths secondhand, insist on thinking for herself, be earnest in her desire to shake off exploded traditions, be untiring in her efforts to live according to a high moral standard and to be strong, and pure, and good—”

===Chinese===
> 「当我的宝宝在就寝时分合上眼睛唱圣歌时，我那疲惫憔悴的灵魂便充满敬畏。种种模糊的回忆涌进我的心头——我自己的母亲和我自己的回忆——多少年前的事了！——想起半睡半醒中被她抱起来、被脱去衣服、被放进小床而不曾惊醒的那份甜美的无助；想起我信过的天使；想起直接从天国降临的小孩子，只要他们乖，就仍被白色翅膀的荫翳环绕——所有这些可爱的诗意胡话，都是当年在她母亲的膝头学来的，正像我的宝宝此刻正在学的一样。她全不知别人讲给她的那些美妙东西有多美，只睁大着天国的眼睛凝望，听母亲讲她方才离开不久的天堂，又被打断这一切的牛奶泡面包宽慰、安抚。两岁时她不懂天使，却懂牛奶泡面包；五岁时她对两者都有了模糊的观念，而更爱牛奶泡面包；十岁时牛奶泡面包和天使都被留在了幼儿室里，她早已发现，它们是她日常生活非必需的奢侈品。再往后几年，她也许会不愿接受二手的真理，坚持自己思考，热切地渴望抖落破了产的旧传统，孜孜不倦地努力按高尚的道德标准生活，做个坚强、纯洁、善良的人——」

===Original===
“Like tea,” explained Irais.

> “—yet will she never, with all her virtues, possess one-thousandth part of the charm that clung about her when she sang, with quiet eyelids, her first reluctant hymns, kneeling on her mother’s knees. I love to come in at bedtime and sit in the window in the setting sunshine watching the mysteries of her going to bed. Her mother tubs her, for she is far too precious to be touched by any nurse, and then she is rolled up in a big bath towel, and only her little pink toes peep out; and when she is powdered, and combed, and tied up in her nightdress, and all her curls are on end, and her ears glowing, she is knelt down on her mother’s lap, a little bundle of fragrant flesh, and her face reflects the quiet of her mother’s face as she goes through her evening prayer for pity and for peace.”

===Chinese===
「——像茶。」伊蕾斯解释道。

> 「——然而，纵有千般美德，她再也企及不了当年那份魅力的千分之一——那时她跪在母亲的膝上，眼睑安详，唱起最初那几句不情愿的圣歌。我爱在就寝时分走进来，坐在窗口的落日余晖里，守望她上床的种种奥秘。她的母亲亲自给她洗澡，因为她贵重得不能让任何保姆沾手；洗完，她被裹进一条大浴巾，只露出小小的粉红脚趾；扑过粉、梳过头、系好睡衣，满头鬈发根根直竖，两只耳朵红扑扑，她被安跪在母亲腿上，一小团香喷喷的小肉团，做晚祷祈求怜悯与安宁时，她的脸映着母亲脸上的宁静。」

===Original===
“How very curious!” said Minora, when she had finished. “That is exactly what I was going to say.”

“Oh, then I have saved you the trouble of putting it together; you can copy that if you like.”

“But have you a stale soul, Miss Minora?” I asked.

“Well, do you know, I rather think that is a good touch,” she replied; “it will make people really think a man wrote the book. You know I am going to take a man’s name.”

“That is precisely what I imagined,” said Irais. “You will call yourself John Jones, or George Potts, or some such sternly commonplace name, to emphasise your uncompromising attitude towards all feminine weaknesses, and no one will be taken in.”

===Chinese===
「多有意思！」米诺拉读完说。「这正是我本来要说的。」

「哦，那我可省了你拼凑的工夫；你乐意就抄走吧。」

「不过你有疲惫憔悴的灵魂吗，米诺拉小姐？」我问。

「嗯，你知道，我倒觉得那是一处妙笔，」她答道；「它会让人们当真以为这书是男人写的。你知道，我打算署一个男人的名字。」

「我想的正是这个，」伊蕾斯说。「你会给自己取名约翰·琼斯，或者乔治·波茨，或者某个这般冷硬乏味的名字，好彰显你对一切女性弱处的决绝态度，而谁也不会上当。」

===Original===
“I really think, Elizabeth,” said Irais to me later, when the click of Minora’s typewriter was heard hesitating in the next room, “that you and I are writing her book for her. She takes down everything we say. Why does she copy all that about the baby? I wonder why mothers’ knees are supposed to be touching? I never learned anything at them, did you? But then in my case they were only stepmother’s, and nobody ever sings their praises.”

“My mother was always at parties,” I said; “and the nurse made me say my prayers in French.”

“And as for tubs and powder,” went on Irais, “when I was a baby such things were not the fashion. There were never any bathrooms, and no tubs; our faces and hands were washed, and there was a foot-bath in the room, and in the summer we had a bath and were put to bed afterwards for fear we might catch cold. My stepmother didn’t worry much; she used to wear pink dresses all over lace, and the older she got the prettier the dresses got. When is she going?”

===Chinese===
「我真觉得，伊丽莎白，」过后伊蕾斯对我说，那时隔壁房间里米诺拉的打字机正嗒嗒犹豫地响着，「你和我是在替她写那本书。我们说的话她句句都记。她干嘛照抄那篇写娃娃的东西？我纳闷，为什么母亲的膝头竟被认为动人？我在那上头什么也没学过，你学过吗？不过我这情况，那终归只是继母的膝头，从没有人歌颂过它们。」

「我母亲永远在赴会，」我说；「保姆教我用法语做祷告。」

「至于浴缸和扑粉，」伊蕾斯继续说，「我做娃娃的时候，这些还不时兴。从来没有浴室，也没有浴缸；我们洗脸洗手，房间里有一只洗脚盆，夏天洗一回澡，洗完立刻被塞上床，生怕着凉。我继母不大操这份心；她常年穿一身缀满蕾丝的粉红裙子，年纪越大，裙子越漂亮。她什么时候走？」

===Original===
“Who? Minora? I haven’t asked her that.”

“Then I will. It is really bad for her art to be neglected like this. She has been here an unconscionable time—it must be nearly three weeks.”

“Yes, she came the same day you did,” I said pleasantly.

Irais was silent. I hope she was reflecting that it is not worse to neglect one’s art than one’s husband, and her husband is lying all this time stretched on a bed of sickness, while she is spending her days so agreeably with me. She has a way of forgetting that she has a home, or any other business in the world than just to stay on chatting with me, and reading, and singing, and laughing at anyone there is to laugh at, and kissing the babies, and tilting with the Man of Wrath. Naturally I love her—she is so pretty that anybody with eyes in his head must love her—but too much of anything is bad, and next month the passages and offices are to be whitewashed, and people who have ever whitewashed their houses inside know what nice places they are to live in while it is being done; and there will be no dinner for Irais, and none of those succulent salads full of caraway seeds that she so devotedly loves. I shall begin to lead her thoughts gently back to her duties by inquiring every day anxiously after her husband’s health. She is not very fond of him, because he does not run and hold the door open for her every time she gets up to leave the room; and though she has asked him to do so, and told him how much she wishes he would, he still won’t. She stayed once in a house where there was an Englishman, and his nimbleness in regard to doors and chairs so impressed her that her husband has had no peace since, and each time she has to go out of a room she is reminded of her disregarded wishes, so that a shut door is to her symbolic of the failure of her married life, and the very sight of one makes her wonder why she was born; at least, that is what she told me once, in a burst of confidence. He is quite a nice, harmless little man, pleasant to talk to, good-tempered, and full of fun; but he thinks he is too old to begin to learn new and uncomfortable ways, and he has that horror of being made better by his wife that distinguishes so many righteous men, and is shared by the Man of Wrath, who persists in holding his glass in his left hand at meals, because if he did not (and I don’t believe he particularly likes doing it) his relations might say that marriage has improved him, and thus drive the iron into his soul. This habit occasions an almost daily argument between one or other of the babies and myself.

===Chinese===
「谁？米诺拉？这我可没问过。」

「那我来问。她的艺术这样被荒废着，实在糟糕。她在这里待的时间长得没道理——怕是有快三个星期了。」

「是啊，她跟你可是同一天来的。」我和颜悦色地说。

伊蕾斯沉默了。我希望她是在反省：荒废自己的艺术固然不好，荒弃自己的丈夫也不好——她的丈夫这一向一直卧病在床，她却在我这里把日子过得这样惬意。她有法子忘掉自己有个家，忘掉世上除了陪我聊天、读书、唱歌、笑话一切可笑的人、亲吻三个宝贝、同愤怒之人斗嘴之外还有别的正事。自然，我爱她——她那样美，凡头上长着眼睛的人都必定爱她——可任何东西过了头都不好，下个月走廊和勤杂用房要刷白了，家里刷过白灰的人都知道施工期间住着是什么滋味；那时候伊蕾斯可没有晚饭吃，也没有她痴爱的那种满是葛缕子籽的美味沙拉了。我要着手温和地引她的心思回归责任：天天焦虑地打听她丈夫的健康。她不大喜欢他，因为她每次起身要出房间，他不肯跑去替她开门；她求过他，也告诉过他她多么希望他这样做，他偏不肯。她有一次在一户人家小住，那里有个英国人，那人在开门和拉椅子上的身手矫健给她印象至深，从此她丈夫再无宁日；每回她要走出房间，她便想起自己被漠视的愿望，于是一扇关着的门，在她那里成了婚姻失败的象征，单是看见一扇，就要让她纳闷自己何苦出生；至少，她有一回推心置腹时是这么对我说的。他其实是个挺不错、无害的小个子男人，谈吐可亲，脾气又好，满肚子诙谐；可他认为自己年纪太大，学不会新的、别扭的做派，而且他怀有那种生怕被妻子改造好的恐惧——多少正经男人都有这恐惧，愤怒之人亦然：他坚持用餐时左手持杯，因为若不这样（我并不信他特别喜欢这样），他的亲戚们也许会说婚姻让他改好了，从而把铁刺进他的灵魂。这个习惯引来三个宝贝中的这一个或那一个与我的几乎每日一辩。

===Original===
“April, hold your glass in your right hand.”

“But papa doesn’t.”

“When you are as old as papa you can do as you like.”

Which was embellished only yesterday by Minora adding impressively, “And only think how strange it would look if *everybody* held their glasses so.”

April was greatly struck by the force of this proposition.

===Chinese===
「四月，杯子要拿在右手。」

「可是爸爸就不。」

「等你长到爸爸那么大，爱怎么拿就怎么拿。」

而就在昨天，这番话还被米诺拉郑重其事地增饰了一句：「再想想看，要是「人人」都这么拿杯子，那该多怪啊。」

四月被这一命题的力量深深打动了。

## January 28th / 1月28日

===Original===
It is very cold—fifteen degrees of frost Réaumur, but perfectly delicious, still, bright weather, and one feels jolly and energetic and amiably disposed towards everybody. The two young ladies are still here, but the air is so buoyant that even they don’t weigh on me any longer, and besides, they have both announced their approaching departure, so that after all I shall get my whitewashing done in peace, and the house will have on its clean pinafore in time to welcome the spring.

===Chinese===
天气很冷——冰点以下列氏十五度（Réaumur），却是极其美妙的晴朗无风，人只觉快活、有劲，对谁都和颜悦色。两位年轻小姐还在这里，但空气那样轻快，连她们也不再压在我心上了；再说，她们俩都已宣布行期将近，所以刷白的工程到底还能安安静静做完，房子也将及时系上干净的围涎，迎接春天。

===Original===
Minora has painted my portrait, and is going to present it as a parting gift to the Man of Wrath; and the fact that I let her do it, and sat meekly times innumerable, proves conclusively, I hope, that I am not vain. When Irais first saw it she laughed till she cried, and at once commissioned her to paint hers, so that she may take it away with her and give it to her husband on his birthday, which happens to be early in February. Indeed, if it were not for this birthday, I really think she would have forgotten to go at all; but birthdays are great and solemn festivals with us, never allowed to slip by unnoticed, and always celebrated in the presence of a sympathetic crowd of relations (gathered from far and near to tell you how well you are wearing, and that nobody would ever dream, and that really it is wonderful), who stand round a sort of sacrificial altar, on which your years are offered up as a burnt-offering to the gods in the shape of lighted pink and white candles, stuck in a very large, flat, jammy cake. The cake with its candles is the chief feature, and on the table round it lie the gifts each person present is more or less bound to give. As my birthday falls in the winter I get mittens as well as blotting-books and photograph-frames, and if it were in the summer I should get photograph-frames and blotting-books and no mittens; but whatever the present may be, and by whomsoever given, it has to be welcomed with the noisiest gratitude, and loudest exclamations of joy, and such words as *entzückend, reizend, herrlich, wundervoll*, and *süss* repeated over and over again, until the unfortunate *Geburtstagskind* feels indeed that another year has gone, and that she has grown older, and wiser, and more tired of folly and of vain repetitions. A flag is hoisted, and all the morning the rites are celebrated, the cake eaten, healths drunk, speeches made, and hands nearly shaken off. The neighbouring parsons drive up, and when nobody is looking their wives count the candles in the cake; the active lady in the next *Schloss* spares time to send a pot of flowers, and to look up my age in the *Gotha Almanach*; a deputation comes from the farms headed by the chief inspector in white kid gloves who invokes Heaven’s blessings on the gracious lady’s head; and the babies are enchanted, and sit in a corner trying on all the mittens. In the evening there is a dinner for the relations and the chief local authorities, with more health-drinking and speechifying, and the next morning, when I come downstairs thankful to have done with it, I am confronted by the altar still in its place, cake crumbs and candle-grease and all, because any hasty removal of it would imply a most lamentable want of sentiment, deplorable in anybody, but scandalous and disgusting in a tender female. All birthdays are observed in this fashion, and not a few wise persons go for a short trip just about the time theirs is due, and I think I shall imitate them next year; only trips to the country or seaside in December are not usually pleasant, and if I go to a town there are sure to be relations in it, and then the cake will spring up mushroom-like from the teeming soil of their affection.

===Chinese===
米诺拉给我画了一幅肖像，打算把它作为临别赠礼送给愤怒之人；而我居然听凭她画，温顺地端坐了无数次，这一事实，我希望，确凿证明我并不虚荣。伊蕾斯头一回见到它时，笑得直掉眼泪，随即委托她再画一幅她自己的，好带回去在她丈夫生日那天送他——他的生日恰好在二月初。说真的，若不是这个生日，我看她连回家都会忘个精光；可生日在我们这里是大而隆重的节庆，从不许悄悄溜过去，总要在一片同情的亲戚面前庆祝——他们从四面八方聚拢来，告诉你你保养得多么好，说你这个年纪谁也猜不着，说这真是了不得——亲戚们环立在一座献祭的祭坛四周，坛上，你的年华正化作点燃的粉白双色蜡烛，插在一个很大很扁、满是果酱的蛋糕上，作为燔祭献给诸神。插着蜡烛的蛋糕是主景，桌上围着它摆开的，是每位在场者多少有义务奉上的礼物。我的生日在冬天，收到的除吸墨纸簿和相框外还有毛线手套；若是夏天，便是相框和吸墨纸簿，没有手套；但无论礼物是什么、出自何人之手，都必须以最喧闹的感激、最大声的欢呼来迎接，*entzückend*（迷人极了）、*reizend*（可爱极了）、*herrlich*（华美极了）、*wundervoll*（奇妙极了）、*süss*（甜极了）这样的字眼要一遍又一遍地重复，直到那不幸的寿星（Geburtstagskind）当真感到又一年过去了，自己老了些，聪明了些，也更厌倦了蠢事和空洞的重复。升起一面旗子，整个上午仪式不断：吃蛋糕、祝酒、致辞，握手握得几乎把手摇下来。邻近的牧师们驱车而来，趁没人看见，他们的太太们就数蛋糕上的蜡烛；隔壁庄园府邸（Schloss）里那位勤快夫人抽空送来一盆花，还在《哥达贵族年鉴》（Gotha Almanach）里查我的年龄；农庄来了一支代表团，由戴白山羊皮手套的总管率领，祈求上天降福于仁慈的夫人头顶；三个宝贝则欢喜疯了，坐在角落里把所有手套挨个试戴。晚上宴请亲戚和当地要员，又是祝酒又是致辞；第二天早晨，当我满怀庆幸地走下楼来，以为一切都过去了，迎面却还是那座原封不动的祭坛，蛋糕屑、烛油一应俱全，因为急急忙忙把它撤走，就意味着可悲地缺乏感情——谁缺这感情都已可叹，在娇柔的女性身上更是可耻又可憎。所有生日都照此办理，不少聪明人便趁自己生日将届之时出门短游，我想明年也学他们；只是十二月的乡下或海边通常不好受，若进城，城里准住着亲戚，蛋糕便会蘑菇似的，从他们深情厚谊的沃土中一夜冒出来。
===Original===
I hope it has been made evident in these pages how superior Irais and myself are to the ordinary weaknesses of mankind; if any further proof were needed, it is furnished by the fact that we both, in defiance of tradition, scorn this celebration of birthday rites. Years ago, when first I knew her, and long before we were either of us married, I sent her a little brass candlestick on her birthday; and when mine followed a few months later, she sent me a notebook. No notes were written in it, and on her next birthday I presented it to her; she thanked me profusely in the customary manner, and when my turn came I received the brass candlestick. Since then we alternately enjoy the possession of each of these articles, and the present question is comfortably settled once and for all, at a minimum of trouble and expense. We never mention this little arrangement except at the proper time, when we send a letter of fervid thanks.

===Chinese===
我希望这些篇章已足以表明，伊蕾斯和我何其超脱于凡人惯常的弱点；倘若还需进一步的证据，那么我们俩公然无视传统、蔑视这套生日仪典，便是明证。多年前我刚认识她时——我们俩都远未出嫁——她生日我送了她一只小黄铜烛台；几个月后我生日，她送了我一本笔记本。笔记本里一个字没写，到她下一个生日，我把它回赠给她；她照惯例对我千恩万谢，轮到我生日，我收到的又是那只黄铜烛台。从此我们轮流享有这两件物品的所有权，送礼问题就此一劳永逸、舒舒服服地解决，麻烦和开销都降到最低。这个小安排我们平日绝口不提，只在应景的时辰，互寄一封热烈的谢函。

===Original===
This radiant weather, when mere living is a joy, and sitting still over the fire out of the question, has been going on for more than a week. Sleighing and skating have been our chief occupation, especially skating, which is more than usually fascinating here, because the place is intersected by small canals communicating with a lake and the river belonging to the lake, and as everything is frozen black and hard, we can skate for miles straight ahead without being obliged to turn round and come back again—at all times an annoying, and even mortifying, proceeding. Irais skates beautifully: modesty is the only obstacle to my saying the same of myself; but I may remark that all Germans skate well, for the simple reason that every year of their lives, for three or four months, they may do it as much as they like. Minora was astonished and disconcerted by finding herself left behind, and arriving at the place where tea meets us half an hour after we had finished. In some places the banks of the canals are so high that only our heads appear level with the fields, and it is, as Minora noted in her book, a curious sight to see three female heads skimming along apparently by themselves, and enjoying it tremendously. When the banks are low, we appear to be gliding deliciously over the roughest ploughed fields, with or without legs according to circumstances. Before we start, I fix on the place where tea and a sleigh are to meet us, and we drive home again; because skating against the wind is as detestable as skating with it is delightful, and an unkind Nature arranges its blowing without the smallest regard for our convenience. Yesterday, by way of a change, we went for a picnic to the shores of the Baltic, icebound at this season, and utterly desolate at our nearest point. I have a weakness for picnics, especially in winter, when the mosquitoes cease from troubling and the anthills are at rest; and of all my many favourite picnic spots this one on the Baltic is the loveliest and best. As it is a three-hours’ drive, the Man of Wrath is loud in his lamentations when the special sort of weather comes which means, as experience has taught him, this particular excursion. There must be deep snow, hard frost, no wind, and a cloudless sky; and when, on waking up, I see these conditions fulfilled, then it would need some very potent reason to keep me from having out a sleigh and going off. It is, I admit, a hard day for the horses; but why have horses if they are not to take you where you want to go to, and at the time you want to go? And why should not horses have hard days as well as everybody else? The Man of Wrath loathes picnics, and has no eye for nature and frozen seas, and is simply bored by a long drive through a forest that does not belong to him; a single turnip on his own place is more admirable in his eyes than the tallest, pinkest, straightest pine that ever reared its snow-crowned head against the setting sunlight. Now observe the superiority of woman, who sees that both are good, and after having gazed at the pine and been made happy by its beauty, goes home and placidly eats the turnip. He went once and only once to this particular place, and made us feel so small by his blasé behaviour that I never invite him now. It is a beautiful spot, endless forest stretching along the shore as far as the eye can reach; and after driving through it for miles you come suddenly, at the end of an avenue of arching trees, upon the glistening, oily sea, with the orange-coloured sails of distant fishing-smacks shining in the sunlight. Whenever I have been there it has been windless weather, and the silence so profound that I could hear my pulses beating. The humming of insects and the sudden scream of a jay are the only sounds in summer, and in winter the stillness is the stillness of death.

===Chinese===
这样明晃晃的天气——光是活着便是快乐，安坐炉边绝无可能——已持续了一个多星期。乘雪橇和滑冰是我们的主要营生，尤其是滑冰，在这里更有一种超乎寻常的魅力，因为庄园被一条条与湖泊及湖源河流相通的小运河纵横贯穿，而万物都冻结得乌黑坚硬，我们可以一往直前地滑上好几英里，不必掉头原路折返——折返这回事，向来恼人，甚至有失体面。伊蕾斯滑得漂亮：至于我，碍于谦逊，不便说同样的话；不过我可以说，德国人个个滑得好，理由很简单：他们一生中每年都有三四个月，想滑多少滑多少。米诺拉发现自己被远远甩在后面，等她到达茶点等候我们的地方，我们已用完半个钟头，她又惊又窘。有些地方运河的岸堤高得只有我们的头露出在田地平面上，正如米诺拉记进书里的那样，三颗女人的脑袋仿佛自个儿贴地飞掠，还飞得心花怒放，是一大奇观。岸堤低的时候，我们看上去就像惬惬意意地滑过最崎岖的犁过的田野，依情况不同，时而有腿，时而没腿。出发前，我先定下茶点和雪橇接应我们的地点，回程便坐车；因为逆风滑冰之可恨，正如顺风滑冰之可喜，而不肯行好的大自然安排风向时，对我们方便与否全然不屑一顾。昨天，为换换花样，我们到波罗的海岸边野餐去了——这个时节海边冰封，离我们最近的那一段更是荒凉透顶。我对野餐有偏爱，尤其冬天的野餐，那时蚊虫不再搅扰，蚁冢也安歇了；在我许许多多心爱的野餐地点中，波罗的海边这一处是最可爱、最好的。由于单程要赶三个钟头的车，每当那种特别的天气出现——经验已教会他，那意味着这一趟出游——愤怒之人便大声哀叹。必须雪厚、霜硬、无风、天上一丝云也没有；一觉醒来，见诸事齐备，那么除非有极重大的事由，什么也拦不住我套雪橇出发。我承认，对马来说是苦的一天；可养马若不是要它们在你想去的时候载你去想去的地方，还养来做什么？马凭什么就不该同旁人一样过几天苦日子？愤怒之人痛恨野餐，对自然和冰封的海毫无眼力，坐着车穿过一片不属于他的森林，长途颠簸只叫他腻烦；他自己地里的一棵芜菁，在他眼里也比顶着落日、昂起积雪华冠的最高最红最挺拔的松树更可赞叹。且看女人的优越：她明白两者皆佳，对松树凝望够、被它的美填满了幸福之后，回家平心静气地把芜菁吃掉。那个地方他去过一次，仅仅一次，他那见惯不惊的做派把我们弄得那样自惭形秽，从此我再也不请他。那是个美丽的地方，无边无尽的森林沿着海岸绵延，望不到头；驱车穿过林子走上好几英里，在拱形树木夹道的尽头，你会猝然见到波光粼粼、油亮亮的大海，远处渔船的橙色船帆在阳光里闪耀。我每次去都赶上无风的天气，寂静深得很，深得能听见自己的脉搏跳动。夏天只有虫子的嗡鸣和松鸦突发的一声尖叫，冬天的静止则是死亡的静止。
===Original===
Every paradise has its serpent, however, and this one is so infested by mosquitoes during the season when picnics seem most natural, that those of my visitors who have been taken there for a treat have invariably lost their tempers, and made the quiet shores ring with their wailing and lamentations. These despicable but irritating insects don’t seem to have anything to do but to sit in multitudes on the sand, waiting for any prey Providence may send them; and as soon as the carriage appears they rise up in a cloud, and rush to meet us, almost dragging us out bodily, and never leave us until we drive away again. The sudden view of the sea from the mossy, pine-covered height directly above it where we picnic; the wonderful stretch of lonely shore with the forest to the water’s edge; the coloured sails in the blue distance; the freshness, the brightness, the vastness—all is lost upon the picnickers, and made worse than indifferent to them, by the perpetual necessity they are under of fighting these horrid creatures. It is nice being the only person who ever goes there or shows it to anybody, but if more people went, perhaps the mosquitoes would be less lean, and hungry, and pleased to see us. It has, however, the advantage of being a suitable place to which to take refractory visitors when they have stayed too long, or left my books out in the garden all night, or otherwise made their presence a burden too grievous to be borne; then one fine hot morning when they are all looking limp, I suddenly propose a picnic on the Baltic. I have never known this proposal fail to be greeted with exclamations of surprise and delight.

===Chinese===
然而每座天堂都有它的蛇，这一处的蛇便是季节一到、野餐正合时宜时铺天盖地的蚊虫，结果凡被我带去享福的客人，无不失去耐性，把静静的海岸闹得哀号四起。这些卑劣而又磨人的虫子似乎百事不理，只成群结队伏在沙地上，等候上天送来的任何猎物；马车一露面，它们便腾起一片乌云，扑上来迎接我们，几乎要把我们整个人拖下车，直到我们再度驱车离开，方才罢休。从我们野餐的、生满苔藓的松冈上骤然望见的大海；森林一直长到水边的孤寂长滩；蓝色远处的彩色船帆；那清新、那明亮、那浩瀚——野餐者对这一切全都无动于衷，甚至心生厌憎，只因他们时刻都被迫同这些可怕的虫豸搏斗。做唯一去那儿、唯一领人去那儿的人是件美事；不过去的人若是多一些，蚊子或许就不至于那样干瘪、那样饥肠辘辘、那样一见我们就眉开眼笑了。然而它另有一桩好处：招待那些住得过久、或把我的书丢在花园里过了一夜、或以别的方式把自身变成不堪重负之负担的桀骜客人，那里是再合适不过的去处；于是在某个晴热的大早晨，趁他们个个蔫头耷脑，我突然提议到波罗的海边野餐。据我所知，这个提议回回都赢得又惊又喜的欢呼。

===Original===
“The Baltic! You never told us you were within driving distance? How *heavenly* to get a breath of sea air on a day like this! The very *thought* puts new life into one! And how *delightful* to see the Baltic! Oh, *please* take us!” And then I take them.

===Chinese===
「波罗的海！您从没说过您家坐车就到海边？这样的天气能吸一口海风，多「如登天堂」啊！光是想一想就让人精神焕发！还能亲眼看看波罗的海，多「令人心醉」！哦，千万「求您」带我们去！」于是我就带他们去。

===Original===
But on a brilliant winter’s day my conscience is as clear as the frosty air itself, and yesterday morning we started off in the gayest of spirits, even Minora being disposed to laugh immoderately on the least provocation. Only our eyes were allowed to peep out from the fur and woollen wrappings necessary to our heads if we would come back with our ears and noses in the same places they were in when we started, and for the first two miles the mirth created by each other’s strange appearance was uproarious—a fact I mention merely to show what an effect dry, bright, intense cold produces on healthy bodies, and how much better it is to go out in it and enjoy it than to stay indoors and sulk. As we passed through the neighbouring village with cracking of whip and jingling of bells, heads popped up at the windows to stare, and the only living thing in the silent, sunny street was a melancholy fowl with ruffled feathers, which looked at us reproachfully, as we dashed with so much energy over the crackling snow.

===Chinese===
可在灿烂的冬日，我的良心同这霜天一样清白，昨天早晨我们兴高采烈地出发了，连米诺拉也动辄毫无节制地发笑。脑袋上裹足毛皮和绒线——若想回来时耳朵鼻子仍各安其位，这行头必不可少——只许眼睛露在外面；头两英里，彼此怪模怪样的尊容引发的欢腾震天动地——我提这一笔，只为说明干爽、明亮、彻骨的严寒对健康的身体有何等功效，出门去享受它又比闷在屋里生闷气强出多少。我们鞭声噼啪、铃铛叮当地穿过邻村，一颗颗脑袋从窗口探出来张望；静悄悄的阳光街面上唯一的活物，是一只羽毛蓬乱、神情忧郁的母鸡，当我们劲头十足地碾过咯吱作响的积雪，它责备地望了我们一眼。

===Original===
“Oh, foolish bird!” Irais called out as we passed; “you’ll be indeed a cold fowl if you stand there motionless, and everyone prefers them hot in weather like this!”

And then we all laughed exceedingly, as though the most splendid joke had been made, and before we had done we were out of the village and in the open country beyond, and could see my house and garden far away behind, glittering in the sunshine; and in front of us lay the forest, with its vistas of pines stretching away into infinity, and a drive through it of fourteen miles before we reached the sea. It was a hoarfrost day, and the forest was an enchanted forest leading into fairyland, and though Irais and I have been there often before, and always thought it beautiful, yet yesterday we stood under the final arch of frosted trees, struck silent by the sheer loveliness of the place. For a long way out the sea was frozen, and then there was a deep blue line, and a cluster of motionless orange sails; at our feet a narrow strip of pale yellow sand; right and left the line of sparkling forest; and we ourselves standing in a world of white and diamond traceries. The stillness of an eternal Sunday lay on the place like a benediction.

===Chinese===
「哦，傻鸟儿！」经过时伊蕾斯喊道；「站在那儿一动不动，你可就真成冻鸡了，这种天气谁不爱吃热鸡呀！」

随后我们全都放声大笑，仿佛刚刚诞生了一个最精彩的笑话，还没笑完，村子已被抛在身后，前面是开阔的原野，回头能望见我的房屋和花园远远地在阳光里闪烁；我们面前横着森林，松林的景深一望无际，要穿过十四英里的林地才到海边。那是一个白霜天，森林是通入仙境的魔法森林；尽管伊蕾斯和我从前常去，一向觉得它美，可昨天我们立在霜树结成的最后一道拱门下，被那纯粹的美击得默然无声。海面向外冻结了很远，然后是一道深蓝色的线，一簇纹丝不动的橙色船帆；我们脚下是一窄条淡黄的沙；左右两侧是晶莹闪烁的林线；而我们自己，立在一个白银与钻石镂花的世界里。一种永恒礼拜日的静谧，祝福般笼罩着这地方。
===Original===
Minora broke the silence by remarking that Dresden was pretty, but she thought this beat it almost.

“I don’t quite see,” said Irais in a hushed voice, as though she were in a holy place, “how the two can be compared.”

“Yes, Dresden is more convenient, of course,” replied Minora; after which we turned away and thought we would keep her quiet by feeding her, so we went back to the sleigh and had the horses taken out and their cloths put on, and they were walked up and down a distant glade while we sat in the sleigh and picnicked. It *is* a hard day for the horses—nearly thirty miles there and back and no stable in the middle; but they are so fat and spoiled that it cannot do them much harm sometimes to taste the bitterness of life. I warmed soup in a little apparatus I have for such occasions, which helped to take the chilliness off the sandwiches—this is the only unpleasant part of a winter picnic, the clammy quality of the provisions just when you most long for something very hot. Minora let her nose very carefully out of its wrappings, took a mouthful, and covered it up quickly again.
