She was nervous lest it should be frost-nipped, and truth compels me to add that her nose is not a bad nose, and might even be pretty on anybody else; but she does not know how to carry it, and there is an art in the angle at which one’s nose is held just as in everything else, and really noses were intended for something besides mere blowing.

===Chinese===
打破沉默的是米诺拉，她评议说，德累斯顿很美，不过她觉得这里几乎胜过它。

「我不大明白，」伊蕾斯轻声说，仿佛置身圣地，「这两者怎么能相提并论。」

「是啊，德累斯顿当然更方便。」米诺拉答道；此后我们便转过身去，心想不如用食物堵住她的嘴，于是回到雪橇旁，卸下马，给它们盖上马衣，牵到远处的林间空地上遛达，我们则坐在雪橇里野餐。对马来说这「确实」是苦的一天——来回将近三十英里，中途没有马厩；可它们养得那样肥、宠得那样惯，偶尔尝尝生活的苦涩，于它们不无好处。我用一副为此类场合置办的小炉子温了汤，好去掉三明治的凉意——这是冬季野餐唯一不痛快的部分：食物又湿又冷，偏偏你那时最渴望滚烫的东西。米诺拉让她的鼻子小心翼翼地从包裹里探出来，吃了一口，又赶紧捂上。她提心吊胆，生怕它冻着——说句实话，她的鼻子不坏，长在别人脸上甚至称得上漂亮；可她不懂得怎么佩戴它。鼻子仰起的角度同万事万物一样，都讲究艺术，再说，鼻子生来毕竟不单是为了擤的。

===Original===
It is the most difficult thing in the world to eat sandwiches with immense fur and woollen gloves on, and I think we ate almost as much fur as anything, and choked exceedingly during the process. Minora was angry at this, and at last pulled off her glove, but quickly put it on again.

“How very unpleasant,” she remarked after swallowing a large piece of fur.

“It will wrap round your pipes, and keep them warm,” said Irais.

“Pipes!” echoed Minora, greatly disgusted by such vulgarity.

“I’m afraid I can’t help you,” I said, as she continued to choke and splutter; “we are all in the same case, and I don’t know how to alter it.”

“There are such things as forks, I suppose,” snapped Minora.

“That’s true,” said I, crushed by the obviousness of the remedy; but of what use are forks if they are fifteen miles off? So Minora had to continue to eat her gloves.

By the time we had finished, the sun was already low behind the trees and the clouds beginning to flush a faint pink. The old coachman was given sandwiches and soup, and while he led the horses up and down with one hand and held his lunch in the other, we packed up—or, to be correct, I packed, and the others looked on and gave me valuable advice.

===Chinese===
戴着肥大的毛皮加绒线手套吃三明治，是天底下最困难的事，我看我们吃下去的毛皮几乎同别的一样多，呛得也格外厉害。米诺拉为此恼火，终于扯下一只手套，可很快又戴了回去。

「真难受。」她咽下一大口毛皮后评论道。

「它会裹住你的管道，给它们保暖。」伊蕾斯说。

「管道！」米诺拉应声道，这般粗鄙令她大为厌恶。

「只怕我帮不了你，」她继续呛咳喷唾时我说；「我们处境相同，我也不知道怎么改变。」

「世上有叉子这种东西吧，我想。」米诺拉厉声说。

「这话不假。」我说，被这疗法的浅显明了压服了；可叉子若在十五英里之外，又有何用？于是米诺拉只好继续吃她的手套。

我们收拾停当时，太阳已经低垂在树后，云彩开始泛起淡淡的红晕。老车夫领了三明治和汤，他一手牵着马来回遛，一手端着自己的午饭；我们则打点行装——准确地说，是我打点，其余人袖手旁观，向我提出宝贵的建议。

===Original===
This coachman, Peter by name, is seventy years old, and was born on the place, and has driven its occupants for fifty years, and I am nearly as fond of him as I am of the sundial; indeed, I don’t know what I should do without him, so entirely does he appear to understand and approve of my tastes and wishes. No drive is too long or difficult for the horses if I want to take it, no place impossible to reach if I want to go to it, no weather or roads too bad to prevent my going out if I wish to: to all my suggestions he responds with the readiest cheerfulness, and smoothes away all objections raised by the Man of Wrath, who rewards his alacrity in doing my pleasure by speaking of him as an *alter Esel*. In the summer, on fine evenings, I love to drive late and alone in the scented forests, and when I have reached a dark part stop, and sit quite still, listening to the nightingales repeating their little tune over and over again after interludes of gurgling, or if there are no nightingales, listening to the marvellous silence, and letting its blessedness descend into my very soul. The nightingales in the forests about here all sing the same tune, and in the same key (E flat)

I don’t know whether all nightingales do this, or if it is peculiar to this particular spot. When they have sung it once, they clear their throats a little, and hesitate, and then do it again, and it is the prettiest little song in the world. How could I indulge my passion for these drives with their pauses without Peter? He is so used to them that he stops now at the right moment without having to be told, and he is ready to drive me all night if I wish it, with no sign of anything but cheerful willingness on his nice old face. The Man of Wrath deplores these eccentric tastes, as he calls them, of mine; but has given up trying to prevent my indulging them because, while he is deploring in one part of the house, I have slipped out at a door in the other, and am gone before he can catch me, and have reached and am lost in the shadows of the forest by the time he has discovered that I am nowhere to be found.

===Chinese===
这位车夫名叫彼得，年届七十，生在庄园，为庄园的主人们赶了五十年车；我喜爱他，几乎同喜爱那座日晷不相上下；真的，没有他我真不知如何是好，因为他看上去完完全全理解并赞许我的趣味和心愿。只要我想走的路，再长再难，马也不嫌；只要我想去的地方，再远也到得了；只要我愿意出门，再坏的天气路况也拦不住：对我的种种提议，他一概报以最爽快的欣然，把愤怒之人提出的异议一一抹平，而愤怒之人酬谢他殷勤伺候我之热忱的方式，是称他一声老驴（alter Esel）。夏天晴朗的傍晚，我爱独自晚归，驱车穿行在香气氤氲的森林里，行到幽暗处便停下，静静坐着，听夜莺在一阵阵咯咯的过门之后，把那支小曲一遍遍重唱；若无夜莺，便听那奇妙的寂静，让它的福泽沁入我灵魂深处。这一带森林里的夜莺唱的都是同一支曲子，同一个调（降E调）。

我不知道是否所有的夜莺都这样，还是这地方独有。它们唱过一遍，清了清嗓子，踌躇一下，再来一遍，那是世界上最可爱的小曲。没有彼得，我这份带着驻足流连的驾车癖好如何满足得了？他已惯熟了这些，如今不必吩咐，就会在恰当的时刻停车；只要我愿意，他随时备好赶我走一整夜，那张和善的老脸上除却乐意的神色，别无他样。愤怒之人把我这些癖好——他这么称呼——叹为怪癖；可他已放弃阻拦我纵情了，因为每当他在房子的这一头叹惋，我早已从那一头的门溜了出去，他还没追上，人已没影，等他发现我无处可寻时，我已到了森林的暗影里，融于其中。

===Original===
The brightness of Peter’s perfections are sullied however by one spot, and that is, that as age creeps upon him, he not only cannot hold the horses in if they don’t want to be held in, but he goes to sleep sometimes on his box if I have him out too soon after lunch, and has upset me twice within the last year—once last winter out of a sleigh, and once this summer, when the horses shied at a bicycle, and bolted into the ditch on one side of the *chaussée* (German for high road), and the bicycle was so terrified at the horses shying that it shied too into the ditch on the other side, and the carriage was smashed, and the bicycle was smashed, and we were all very unhappy, except Peter, who never lost his pleasant smile, and looked so placid that my tongue clave to the roof of my mouth when I tried to make it scold him.

===Chinese===
然而，彼得完美品性的光辉上有一个污点：随着年岁悄悄爬上他，马若不肯收住，他不但勒不住，而且倘若我午饭后过早唤他出车，他有时会在车座上打瞌睡；过去一年里他已把我翻出去两回——一回是去年冬天，从雪橇里；一回是今年夏天，马见了自行车受惊，一头窜进大路（chaussée，法语借词，德语也用）这一边的沟里，那自行车被马一惊也受了惊，闪进了另一边的沟，马车摔烂了，自行车也摔烂了，我们全都很难过，唯独彼得例外，他脸上那愉快的微笑始终不褪，神情安详得叫我想斥责他时，舌头都粘在了上膛。
===Original===
“But I should think he ought to have been *thoroughly* scolded on an occasion like that,” said Minora, to whom I had been telling this story as we wandered on the yellow sands while the horses were being put in the sleigh; and she glanced nervously up at Peter, whose mild head was visible between the bushes above us. “Shall we get home before dark?” she asked.

The sun had altogether disappeared behind the pines and only the very highest of the little clouds were still pink; out at sea the mists were creeping up, and the sails of the fishing-smacks had turned a dull brown; a flight of wild geese passed across the disc of the moon with loud cacklings.

“Before dark?” echoed Irais, “I should think not. It is dark now nearly in the forest, and we shall have the loveliest moonlight drive back.”

“But it is surely very dangerous to let a man who goes to sleep drive you,” said Minora apprehensively.

“But he’s such an old dear,” I said.

“Yes, yes, no doubt,” she replied testily; “but there are wakeful old dears to be had, and on a box they are preferable.”

Irais laughed. “You are growing quite amusing, Miss Minora,” she said.

===Chinese===
「可依我看，那样的场合他「理应」挨一顿痛骂。」米诺拉说——套马的当儿，我们在黄色的沙地上漫步，我正给她讲这段故事；她不安地抬头瞥了一眼彼得，他那颗温顺的脑袋在我们上方的灌木间露出。「我们天黑前能到家吗？」她问。

太阳已完全没入松林背后，只剩最高处的几小片云还是粉的；海面上雾气悄悄爬起，渔船的帆已转成暗褐色；一行大雁嘎嘎高叫着掠过月轮。

「天黑前？」伊蕾斯应道，「我看不能。林子里现在就差不多黑了，我们正好赶上最可爱的月光回程。」

「可是让一个会睡着的人赶车，想必很危险。」米诺拉忧虑地说。

「可他是位多么可爱的老人家呀。」我说。

「是，是，毫无疑问，」她烦躁地答道；「可是醒着的老人家也找得到，坐上车座，还是他们更可取。」

伊蕾斯笑了。「你越来越有趣了，米诺拉小姐。」她说。

===Original===
“He isn’t on a box today,” said I; “and I never knew him to go to sleep standing up behind us on a sleigh.” But Minora was not to be appeased, and muttered something about seeing no fun in foolhardiness, which shows how alarmed she was, for it was rude.

Peter, however, behaved beautifully on the way home, and Irais and I at least were as happy as possible driving back, with all the glories of the western sky flashing at us every now and then at the end of a long avenue as we swiftly passed, and later on, when they had faded, myriads of stars in the narrow black strip of sky over our heads. It was bitterly cold, and Minora was silent, and not in the least inclined to laugh with us as she had been six hours before.

“ ‘Have you enjoyed yourself, Miss Minora?’ inquired Irais, as we got out of the forest on to the *chaussée*, and the lights of the village before ours twinkled in the distance.

“How many degrees do you suppose there are now?” was Minora’s reply to this question.

“Degrees?—Of frost? Oh, dear me, are you cold,” cried Irais solicitously.

“Well, it isn’t exactly warm, is it?” said Minora sulkily; and Irais pinched me. “Well, but think how much colder you would have been without all that fur you ate for lunch inside you,” she said.

“And what a nice chapter you will be able to write about the Baltic,” said I. “Why, it is practically certain that you are the first English person who has ever been to just this part of it.”

“Isn’t there some English poem,” said Irais, “about being the first who ever burst—”

“‘Into that silent sea,’ ” finished Minora hastily. “You can’t quote that without its context, you know.”

“But I wasn’t going to,” said Irais meekly; “I only paused to breathe. I must breathe, or perhaps I might die.”

===Chinese===
「他今天不坐车座，」我说；「而且我从来没见他站在雪橇后头睡着过。」可米诺拉不肯消气，咕哝了一句什么蛮勇一点乐趣也没有的话——这话表明她吓成了什么样，因为这话很失礼。

不过，彼得在归路上表现得无可挑剔，至少伊蕾斯和我回去时快活到了极点：每当疾驰着掠过一条长长的林荫道的尽头，西天全部的壮丽便不时朝我们闪现，待它们褪尽之后，头顶那一窄条黑色天空里又缀满万千星子。天气冷得刺骨，米诺拉沉默着，全无六小时前同我们一同欢笑的意思了。

「『你玩得开心吗，米诺拉小姐？』伊蕾斯问道；这时我们已驶出森林，上了大路（chaussée），我们村前面那座村子的灯火在远处闪烁。

「你猜现在有几度？」米诺拉用这一句答了那一问。

「度？——零下的度？哎呀天哪，你冷吗？」伊蕾斯关切地喊道。

「嗯，这天气总归算不上暖和吧？」米诺拉赌气地说；伊蕾斯掐了我一把。「可是想想看，要不是午饭吃下去那么多毛皮垫着，你还要冷成什么样呢。」她说。

「再说，你可以写一篇多好的波罗的海章节啊。」我说。「哎呀，几乎可以肯定，你是头一个到过这一段海岸的英国人。」

「有没有那么一句英国诗，」伊蕾斯说，「说的是做第一个闯进——」

「『那片寂静的海』。」米诺拉急忙接完。「你知道，引这句不能不带上下文。」

「可我没打算引。」伊蕾斯乖顺地说；「我只是停下来喘口气。我总得喘气，不然没准会死。」

===Original===
The lights from my energetic friend’s *Schloss* shone brightly down upon us as we passed round the base of the hill on which it stands; she is very proud of this hill, as well she may be, seeing that it is the only one in the whole district.

“Do you never go there?” asked Minora, jerking her head in the direction of the house.

“Sometimes. She is a very busy woman, and I should feel I was in the way if I went often.”

“It would be interesting to see another North German interior,” said Minora; “and I should be obliged if you would take me.”

“But I can’t fall upon her suddenly with a strange girl,” I protested; “and we are not at all on such intimate terms as to justify my taking all my visitors to see her.”

“What do you want to see another interior for?” asked Irais. “I can tell you what it is like; and if you went nobody would speak to you, and if you were to ask questions, and began to take notes, the good lady would stare at you in the frankest amazement, and think Elizabeth had brought a young lunatic out for an airing. *Everybody* is not as patient as Elizabeth,” added Irais, anxious to pay off old scores.

“I would do a great deal for you, Miss Minora,” I said, “but I can’t do that.”

===Chinese===
绕过山脚时，我那位精力过人的朋友的庄园府邸（Schloss）的灯火明亮地照临我们；她为这座山十分自豪，也理直气壮，因为它是整个地区独一座。

「你从不去那儿吗？」米诺拉问，朝那宅子的方向一甩头。

「偶尔去。她是位大忙人，去勤了我该觉得自己碍事了。」

「见识另一户北德人家的内宅会很有意思，」米诺拉说；「您若肯带我去，我将不胜感激。」

「可我不能冷不丁带个陌生姑娘闯到她家，」我抗议道；「我们的交情也远远没亲密到可以领我所有的客人去见她的地步。」

「你要看人家的内宅做什么？」伊蕾斯问。「我可以讲给你听它什么样；你若是去了，没人会搭理你；你若是发问，还记起笔记来，那位好夫人会用最坦率的惊愕瞪着你，心想伊丽莎白领了个年轻疯子出来放风。「不是人人」都像伊丽莎白这样有耐性的。」伊蕾斯补上一句，急着要报旧怨。

「为你我什么都肯做，米诺拉小姐，」我说，「唯独这个不成。」
===Original===
“If we went,” said Irais, “Elizabeth and I would be placed with great ceremony on a sofa behind a large, polished oval table with a crochet-mat in the centre—it *has* got a crochet-mat in the centre, hasn’t it?” I nodded. “And you would sit on one of the four little podgy, buttony, tasselly red chairs that are ranged on the other side of the table facing the sofa. They *are* red, Elizabeth?” Again I nodded. “The floor is painted yellow, and there is no carpet except a rug in front of the sofa. The paper is dark chocolate colour, almost black; that is in order that after years of use the dirt may not show, and the room need not be done up. Dirt is like wickedness, you see, Miss Minora—its being there never matters; it is only when it shows so much as to be apparent to everybody that we are ashamed of it. At intervals round the high walls are chairs, and cabinets with lamps on them, and in one corner is a great white cold stove—or is it majolica?” she asked, turning to me.

“No, it is white.”

“There are a great many lovely big windows, all ready to let in the air and the sun, but they are as carefully covered with brown lace curtains under heavy stuff ones as though a whole row of houses were just opposite, with peering eyes at every window trying to look in, instead of there only being fields, and trees, and birds. No fire, no sunlight, no books, no flowers; but a consoling smell of red cabbage coming up under the door, mixed, in due season, with soapsuds.”

“When did you go there?” asked Minora.

“Ah, when did I go there indeed? When did I not go there? I have been calling there all my life.”

Minora’s eyes rolled doubtfully first at me then at Irais from the depths of her head-wrappings; they are large eyes with long dark eyelashes, and far be it from me to deny that each eye taken by itself is fine, but they are put in all wrong.

===Chinese===
「倘若我们去，」伊蕾斯说，「伊丽莎白和我将被以隆重的礼节安置在沙发上，沙发前是一张擦得锃亮的大椭圆桌，正中一块钩针编织垫——它正中「确实」有块钩针垫，对吧？」我点头。「而你呢，将坐在沿桌子另一侧、与沙发面对面排开的四把矮胖、缀满纽扣和流苏的小红椅中的一把上。它们「确实」是红的吧，伊丽莎白？」我又点头。「地板漆成黄色，除了沙发前一块小毯，没有地毯。墙纸是深巧克力色，近乎黑；这是为了用上多年之后脏了也看不出来，房间便不必翻新。脏同恶一样，你明白吗，米诺拉小姐——它在那里从不打紧；只有当它显形显到人人都看得见时，我们才为它害臊。高高的四壁间隔着摆椅子和顶上带灯的橱柜，一个墙角立着一座白色的大冷炉子——还是彩釉陶的来着？」她转向我问。

「不，是白色的。」

「还有许许多多可爱的大窗，全都随时准备放空气和阳光进来，却被褐色蕾丝帘外加厚呢帘层层遮护得严严实实，仿佛对面正立着一整排房子，每扇窗里都有窥探的眼睛想往里看似的，全不顾外面只有田野、树木和鸟雀。没有炉火，没有阳光，没有书，没有花；只有一股慰藉人的紫甘蓝气味从门底下透上来，到了时节，还掺着肥皂水味。」

「你什么时候去过那儿？」米诺拉问。

「啊，我到底是什么时候去的？我什么时候没去过？我这辈子一直在那儿拜访。」

米诺拉的眼睛从头巾深处将信将疑地先滚向我、再滚向伊蕾斯；那是双大眼睛，睫毛又长又黑，我决不肯否认，单取一只来看都不错，可它们装配得全不对。

===Original===
“The only thing you would learn there,” went on Irais, “would be the significance of sofa corners in Germany. If we three went there together, I should be ushered into the right-hand corner of the sofa, because it is the place of honour, and I am the greatest stranger; Elizabeth would be invited to seat herself in the left-hand corner, as next in importance; the hostess would sit near us in an armchair; and you, as a person of no importance whatever, would either be left to sit where you could, or would be put on a chair facing us, and with the entire breadth of the table between us to mark the immense social gulf that separates the married woman from the mere virgin. These sofa corners make the drawing of nice distinctions possible in a way that nothing else could. The world might come to an end, and create less sensation in doing it, than you would, Miss Minora, if by any chance you got into the right-hand corner of one. That you are put on a chair on the other side of the table places you at once in the scale of precedence, and exactly defines your social position, or rather your complete want of a social position.” And Irais tilted her nose ever so little heavenwards.

“Note it,” she added, “as the heading of your next chapter.”

“Note what?” asked Minora impatiently.

===Chinese===
「你在那儿唯一能学到的东西，」伊蕾斯继续说，「是德国沙发角落的意义。倘若我们三个一同前往，我将荣居沙发的右手角，因为那是上座，而我是最尊贵的生客；伊丽莎白将受邀坐进左手角，位列第二；女主人坐在我们近旁的扶手椅里；而你，作为一个全然无足轻重的人，要么听便自己哪儿能坐坐哪儿，要么被安在正对着我们的一把椅子上，让我们与你之间隔着整张桌子的宽度，以标明隔开已婚妇人与小处女的那道社会鸿沟。这些沙发角落使精细的等级区分成为可能，旁的东西谁也办不到。世界就算到了末日，掀起的轰动也不见得比你大，米诺拉小姐——倘若你不巧坐进了哪张沙发的右手角。把你放在桌子另一侧的椅子上，立刻就把你在尊卑序列上定了位，精确界定出你的社会地位——或者毋宁说，你社会地位的全然阙如。」说罢，伊蕾斯把鼻子朝天微微翘了那么一点儿。

「记下来，」她补一句，「当你下一章的标题。」

「记什么？」米诺拉不耐烦地问。

===Original===
“Why, ‘The Subtle Significance of Sofas,’ of course,” replied Irais. “If,” she continued, as Minora made no reply appreciative of this suggestion, “you were to call unexpectedly, the bad luck which pursues the innocent would most likely make you hit on a washing-day, and the distracted mistress of the house would keep you waiting in the cold room so long while she changed her dress, that you would begin to fear you were to be left to perish from want and hunger; and when she did appear, would show by the bitterness of her welcoming smile the rage that was boiling in her heart.”

“But what has the mistress of the house to do with washing?”

“What has she to do with washing? Oh, you sweet innocent—pardon my familiarity, but such ignorance of country-life customs is very touching in one who is writing a book about them.”

“Oh, I have no doubt I am very ignorant,” said Minora loftily.

===Chinese===
「当然是《沙发的微妙意涵》喽。」伊蕾斯答道。「倘若，」见米诺拉对这一建议毫无领教的表示，她接着说，「你不告而至，追猎无辜者的厄运十有八九会让你撞上洗涤日，心慌意乱的女主人换衣服，让你在冷屋子里久等，等到你开始担心自己将饥寒交迫而死；等她终于露面，欢迎的笑里那股苦味，会把她心中翻腾的怒火泄露无遗。」

「可女主人和洗涤有什么相干？」

「她跟洗涤有什么相干？哦，你这甜美的小天真——恕我失礼，不过对乡俗这般无知，出在一位正写乡俗大著的人身上，实在感人。」

「哦，我毫不怀疑我很无知。」米诺拉傲然说。

===Original===
“Seasons of washing,” explained Irais, “are seasons set apart by the *Hausfrau* to be kept holy. They only occur every two or three months, and while they are going on the whole house is in an uproar, every other consideration sacrificed, husband and children sunk into insignificance, and no one approaching, or interfering with the mistress of the house during these days of purification, but at their peril.”

“You don’t really mean,” said Minora, “that you only wash your clothes four times a year?”

“Yes, I do mean it,” replied Irais.

“Well, I think that is very disgusting,” said Minora emphatically.

Irais raised those pretty, delicate eyebrows of hers. “Then you must take care and not marry a German,” she said.

“But what is the object of it?” went on Minora.

“Why, to clean the linen, I suppose.”

“Yes, yes, but why only at such long intervals?”

“It is an outward and visible sign of vast possessions in the shape of linen. If you were to want to have your clothes washed every week, as you do in England, you would be put down as a person who only has just enough to last that length of time, and would be an object of general contempt.”

“But I should be a clean object,” cried Minora, “and my house would not be full of accumulated dirt.”

We said nothing—there was nothing to be said.

“It must be a happy land, that England of yours,” Irais remarked after a while with a sigh—a beatific vision no doubt presenting itself to her mind of a land full of washerwomen and agile gentlemen darting at doorhandles.

“It is a *clean* land, at any rate,” replied Minora.

“*I* don’t want to go and live in it,” said I—for we were driving up to the house, and a memory of fogs and umbrellas came into my mind as I looked up fondly at its dear old west front, and I felt that what I want is to live and die just here, and that there never was such a happy woman as Elizabeth.

===Chinese===
「洗涤季，」伊蕾斯解说，「是家庭主妇（Hausfrau）分别为圣的时节。每两三个月才逢一回，洗涤季一开张，全家上下人仰马翻，其余一切概可牺牲，丈夫孩子沦为无物，谁在这净化的日子里走近女主人、打搅女主人，谁就自担风险。」

「你不会当真是说，」米诺拉说，「你们一年只洗四次衣服？」

「是，我当真是这个意思。」伊蕾斯答。

「哼，我觉得这非常恶心。」米诺拉加重语气说。

伊蕾斯挑起她那两道秀气精致的眉毛。「那你可得当心，千万别嫁给德国人。」她说。

「可这么做到底图什么？」米诺拉追问。

「还能图什么，把亚麻衣物洗干净呗。」

「是，是，可为什么隔这么久才洗一回？」

「那是亚麻家当丰厚的有形外证。你若像在英格兰那样要每星期洗一回衣服，人家便会断定你统共只有撑那么些日子的存货，你将沦为众人鄙夷的对象。」

「可我是个干净的对象呀，」米诺拉喊道，「我的房子也不会积满陈年的脏。」

我们什么也没说——无话可说。

「你那个英格兰，想必是片幸福的乐土。」过了一会儿，伊蕾斯叹口气说——一幅极乐的图景无疑正在她脑海里展开：满地的洗衣妇，还有身手敏捷、直扑门把手的绅士们。

「至少，它是一片「干净」的土地。」米诺拉答。

「「我」可不想去住在那儿。」我说——我们已经驶到屋前，仰望它那可爱的老西山墙时，雾和雨伞的记忆涌上心头，我感到，我要的就是在这里生、在这里死，世上再没有第二个女人比伊丽莎白更幸福了。

## April 18th / 4月18日

===Original===
I have been so busy ever since Irais and Minora left that I can hardly believe the spring is here, and the garden hurrying on its green and flowered petticoat—only its petticoat as yet, for though the underwood is a fairyland of tender little leaves, the trees above are still quite bare.

===Chinese===
自从伊蕾斯和米诺拉走后，我一直忙得不可开交，简直不敢相信春天已经来了，花园正匆匆套上它那缀花的绿衬裙——眼下还只是衬裙，因为虽说林下灌木已是一片嫩叶的仙境，头顶的树却还光秃秃的。

===Original===
February was gone before I well knew that it had come, so deeply was I engaged in making hotbeds, and having them sown with petunias, verbenas, and nicotina affinis; while no less than thirty are dedicated solely to vegetables, it having been borne in upon me lately that vegetables must be interesting things to grow, besides possessing solid virtues not given to flowers, and that I might as well take the orchard and kitchen garden under my wing. So I have rushed in with all the zeal of utter inexperience, and my February evenings were spent poring over gardening books, and my days in applying the freshly absorbed wisdom. Who says that February is a dull, sad, slow month in the country? It was of the cheerfullest, swiftest description here, and its mild days enabled me to get on beautifully with the digging and manuring, and filled my rooms with snowdrops. The longer I live the greater is my respect and affection for manure in all its forms, and already, though the year is so young, a considerable portion of its pin-money has been spent on artificial manure. The Man of Wrath says he never met a young woman who spent her money that way before; I remarked that it must be nice to have an original wife; and he retorted that the word original hardly described me, and that the word eccentric was the one required. Very well, I suppose I am eccentric, since even my husband says so; but if my eccentricities are of such a practical nature as to result later in the biggest cauliflowers and tenderest lettuce in Prussia, why then he ought to be the first to rise up and call me blessed.

===Chinese===
二月还没等我弄清它来了，就又走了，我一头扎进温床里扎得那样深——温床上播了矮牵牛、美女樱和花烟草（nicotina affinis）的种子；还有整整三十张温床专种蔬菜，因为我近来顿悟：种蔬菜想必也是有滋味的事，何况它们还具备鲜花所没有的扎实美德，我不妨索性把果园和菜园也收归羽翼之下。于是我怀着全然无知者的满腔热忱一头冲了进去，二月的夜晚用来啃园艺书，白天用来实践刚吸收的新鲜智慧。谁说二月在乡下是个沉闷、凄清、迟缓的月份？在这里，它简直快活无比、飞快无比，它那些温和的白日让我把翻地施肥的活计干得漂漂亮亮，还让我的房间里插满了雪滴花。活得越久，我对各种形态的肥料就越是敬重爱戴；年头还这么嫩，我的私房零用钱却已有一大笔花在了人造肥料上。愤怒之人说，他从没见过哪个年轻女人这样花钱；我说，娶一位别出心裁的太太想必很妙；他反唇相讥道，「别出心裁」一词还不足以形容我，需要的那个词是「古怪」。好吧，我想我是古怪，连我丈夫都这么说了；可倘若我的古怪实用到日后能结出普鲁士最大的花椰菜、最嫩的莴苣，那么他理当头一个站起来称颂我有福。

===Original===
I sent to England for vegetable-marrow seeds, as they are not grown here, and people try and make boiled cucumbers take their place; but boiled cucumbers are nasty things, and I don’t see why marrows should not do here perfectly well. These, and primrose-roots, are the English contributions to my garden. I brought over the roots in a tin box last time I came from England, and am anxious to see whether they will consent to live here. Certain it is that they don’t exist in the Fatherland, so I can only conclude the winter kills them, for surely, if such lovely things would grow, they never would have been overlooked. Irais is deeply interested in the experiment; she reads so many English books, and has heard so much about primroses, and they have got so mixed up in her mind with leagues, and dames, and Disraelis, that she longs to see this mysterious political flower, and has made me promise to telegraph when it appears, and she will come over. But they are not going to do anything this year, and I only hope those cold days did not send them off to the Paradise of flowers. I am afraid their first impression of Germany was a chilly one.

===Chinese===
我写信到英格兰买西葫芦种子，因为本地不种这东西，人们便试着拿煮黄瓜充数；可煮黄瓜是难吃的东西，我看不出西葫芦为什么不能在这里长得好好的。它们和报春花的根，是我花园收到的英格兰馈赠。上次从英格兰回来，我用一只铁皮盒把根带了过来，正焦急地等着看它们肯不肯在这里活下来。可以肯定的是，祖国（Fatherland）没有这种花，所以我只能断定是冬天把它们冻死的；因为倘若这么可爱的东西肯长，它们断不至于被人忽视。伊蕾斯对这项实验深感兴趣；她读了那么多英国书，听了那么多报春花的事，这花在她心里同「同盟」「贵妇」「迪斯累里」们缠夹得难解难分（指英国的「报春花同盟」，迪斯累里的政治纪念组织），以致她渴望一睹这朵神秘的政治之花，还让我答应，花一开就打电报，她便立刻过来。可它们今年是开不了花了，我只盼前些日子那几场冷天没有把它们打发去花朵的乐园。恐怕它们对德国的第一印象，是个冷冰冰的印象。

===Original===
Irais writes about once a week, and inquires after the garden and the babies, and announces her intention of coming back as soon as the numerous relations staying with her have left—“which they won’t do,” she wrote the other day, “until the first frosts nip them off, when they will disappear like belated dahlias—double ones of course, for single dahlias are too charming to be compared to relations. I have every sort of cousin and uncle and aunt here, and here they have been ever since my husband’s birthday—not the same ones exactly, but I get so confused that I never know where one ends and the other begins. My husband goes off after breakfast to look at his crops, he says, and I am left at their mercy. I wish I had crops to go and look at—I should be grateful even for one, and would look at it from morning till night, and quite stare it out of countenance, sooner than stay at home and have the truth told me by enigmatic aunts. Do you know my Aunt Bertha? she, in particular, spends her time propounding obscure questions for my solution. I get so tired and worried trying to guess the answers, which are always truths supposed to be good for me to hear. ‘Why do you wear your hair on your forehead?’ she asks—and that sets me off wondering why I *do* wear it on my forehead, and what she wants to know for, or whether she does know and only wants to know if I will answer truthfully. ‘I am sure I don’t know, aunt,’ I say meekly, after puzzling over it for ever so long; ‘perhaps my maid knows. Shall I ring and ask her?’ And then she informs me that I wear it so to hide an ugly line she says I have down the middle of my forehead, and that betokens a listless and discontented disposition. Well, if she knew, what did she ask me for? Whenever I am with them they ask me riddles like that, and I simply lead a *dog’s* life. Oh, my dear, relations are like drugs—useful sometimes, and even pleasant, if taken in small quantities and seldom, but dreadfully pernicious on the whole, and the truly wise avoid them.”

===Chinese===
伊蕾斯大约每星期来一封信，问候花园和三个宝贝，并宣布她一等住她家的那许多亲戚走光就回来——「但他们才不走呢，」她前几天写道，「不到初霜把他们掐掉，他们是不会散的，到时候他们会像迟开的大丽花一样无影无踪——当然是重瓣的那种，因为单瓣大丽花太可爱了，不配拿来比亲戚。我这里各色表亲、叔伯、姑姨应有尽有，而且从我丈夫生日起就一直在——不全是同一批人，可我搅得那样糊涂，从来分不清哪个了、哪个起。我丈夫吃过早饭就出门去看他的庄稼，他说，我就任凭他们摆布。我真希望我也有庄稼可看——哪怕只有一垄我也感激不尽，我会从早到晚盯着它，把它盯得面红耳赤，也强过待在家里听打哑谜的姨妈们向我宣讲真理。你认识我的伯莎姨妈吗？她尤其把时间都用来给我出晦涩的题目求解。我猜那些答案猜得又累又烦，而那些答案永远是有益身心的真理。『你为什么把头发留在前额上？』她问——这一问就引得我纳闷我「到底」为什么把头发留在这额前，她问这个做什么，还是她其实知道，只想看看我会不会照实回答。『我真不知道，姨妈，』苦思冥想了半天之后，我温顺地说，『兴许我的女仆知道。要不要我摇铃问问她？』于是她告诉我，我这样留头发是为了遮住她说我额心的一道丑纹，那道纹昭示着一种倦怠而不满的性情。好嘛，她既然知道，还问我做什么？每逢我跟他们在一起，他们就出这样的谜语给我猜，我简直过着「狗」日子。哦亲爱的，亲戚就像药物——小量、偶尔服之，有时有益，甚至愉快，可总体上为害极烈，真正的智者敬而远之。」

===Original===
From Minora I have only had one communication since her departure, in which she thanked me for her pleasant visit, and said she was sending me a bottle of English embrocation to rub on my bruises after skating; that it was wonderful stuff, and she was sure I would like it; and that it cost two marks, and would I send stamps. I pondered long over this. Was it a parting hit, intended as revenge for our having laughed at her? Was she personally interested in the sale of embrocation? Or was it merely Minora’s idea of a graceful return for my hospitality? As for bruises, nobody who skates decently regards it as a bruise-producing exercise, and whenever there were any they were all on Minora; but she did happen to turn round once, I remember, just as I was in the act of tumbling down for the first and only time, and her delight was but thinly veiled by her excessive solicitude and sympathy. I sent her the stamps, received the bottle, and resolved to let her drop out of my life; I had been a good Samaritan to her at the request of my friend, but the best of Samaritans resents the offer of healing oil for his own use. But why waste a thought on Minora at Easter, the real beginning of the year in defiance of calendars. She belongs to the winter that is past, to the darkness that is over, and has no part or lot in the life I shall lead for the next six months. Oh, I could dance and sing for joy that the spring is here! What a resurrection of beauty there is in my garden, and of brightest hope in my heart! The whole of this radiant Easter day I have spent out of doors, sitting at first among the windflowers and celandines, and then, later, walking with the babies to the Hirschwald, to see what the spring had been doing there; and the afternoon was so hot that we lay a long time on the turf, blinking up through the leafless branches of the silver birches at the soft, fat little white clouds floating motionless in the blue. We had tea on the grass in the sun, and when it began to grow late, and the babies were in bed, and all the little windflowers folded up for the night, I still wandered in the green paths, my heart full of happiest gratitude. It makes one very humble to see oneself surrounded by such a wealth of beauty and perfection *anonymously* lavished, and to think of the infinite meanness of our own grudging charities, and how displeased we are if they are not promptly and properly appreciated. I do sincerely trust that the benediction that is always awaiting me in my garden may by degrees be more deserved, and that I may grow in grace, and patience, and cheerfulness, just like the happy flowers I so much love.

===Chinese===
米诺拉走后只来过一次音信，信里谢我让她度过了愉快的做客时光，说她要寄我一瓶英国擦剂，供我滑冰后擦淤伤用；说那东西灵验得很，她笃定我会喜欢；还说它值两马克，问可否寄邮票给她。我为此琢磨了很久。这是临别一击，为我们笑话过她而施的报复？她个人在擦剂的买卖里有份？抑或这仅仅是米诺拉对优雅回报我的款待的理解？至于淤伤，滑冰滑得像样的人谁也不把它当成一项制造淤伤的运动，真有淤伤的时候，也全在米诺拉身上；不过我记得，她倒真有一次恰好回过头来，正赶上我生平头一回也是唯一一回摔个正着，她那份过度的关切和同情，只薄薄遮着一层欢喜。我给她寄了邮票，收到那瓶擦剂，并打定主意让她从我的生活里淡出；我受朋友之托，对她行了撒玛利亚人的好事，可再好的撒玛利亚人，也不愿人家把疗伤的油送来给他自己用。可何必在复活节为米诺拉浪费心思——复活节才是真正的一年之始，日历说了不算。她属于已然逝去的冬天，属于已然过去的黑暗，与我未来六个月的生活无份无缘。哦，春天来了，我能欢喜得又舞又唱！我的花园里有着怎样一场美的复活，我心里又有着怎样一场最明亮的希望的复活！这个光辉灿烂的复活节，我整天都在户外，起初坐在银莲花和白屈菜之间，随后带着三个宝贝步行去希尔施瓦尔德（Hirschwald，鹿林），看春天在那里做成了什么；下午热得很，我们在草地上躺了许久，透过白桦光秃秃的枝条，眯眼望着蓝色天幕上纹丝不动飘浮着的柔软胖乎的白色小云朵。我们在阳光下坐在草地上用了茶；天色渐晚，宝贝们都上了床，小小的银莲花朵朵合拢过夜，我仍在绿色的小径上流连，心中满怀最幸福的感激。看着自己被这样一笔「无名」挥霍的美与完美的财富所环绕，再想想我们自己的施与何其吝啬小气、人家若不即刻得体地领情我们便何等不悦——这叫人变得非常谦卑。我真诚地祈望，那永远在我花园里等候我的祝福，能渐渐变得更为我应得；也愿我在恩典、忍耐与欢畅中成长，就像我如此深爱的那些幸福的花儿。
